# THE PUBLISHER TO THE READER.

*THOSE who desire such rules and advice as may lead them through the stormy sea of life and secure their eternal happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note>, may find in the instructions laid down in these discourses <note>original: "discourses"; here referring to formal religious or philosophical treatises</note> the way to discern the true nature of good and evil, of virtue and vice: that there is no real good but* GOD, *no real evil but sin, and that the only way to attain holiness and*

<summary>This preface introduces a collection of religious teachings designed to guide the reader toward eternal salvation by distinguishing between virtue and vice. It establishes the central theme that true goodness is found only in God, while true evil is found only in sin.</summary>
<keywords>morality, virtue, vice, God, sin, holiness, spiritual guidance, salvation</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the publisher's preface from page iii. The sentence begins on the previous page with "attain holiness and" and concludes here with "Christian virtue."</meta>

*Christian virtue, is to restrain and regulate one’s outward senses, lower reason, and the will and understanding. By doing this, one makes way for the Spirit of* GOD, *which will then take possession of souls prepared in this way and live within them. We are to imitate* JESUS CHRIST *by mortifying <note>original: "mortifying"; the spiritual practice of self-denial or subduing bodily desires and the ego</note> ourselves and entirely surrendering our will and understanding to the will and light of* GOD *through a close communion with him in love. What can make a person as happy as having their love fixed upon an object that will never fail or disappoint them? For if we are united to* GOD, *he—being eternal—cannot fail. His goodness will give us not only appropriate but even undeserved rewards; his truth will ease us of all our jealousies and fears; his omnipotence <note>original: "omnipotence"; all-encompassing power</note> will secure our hope and bring life to our faith; his omniscience <note>original: "omniscience"; all-knowing nature</note> will enlighten our understanding; and his presence will satisfy our thirsty and craving desires with foretastes <note>original: "antepasts"</note> of eternal joy and happiness.*

*For if our internal state is preserved from anxiety and turmoil, and if we quench the raging*

<summary>This page explains that Christian virtue is achieved by subduing the senses and the will to allow the Spirit of God to dwell within the soul. It argues that true happiness comes from a loving union with God, whose eternal attributes provide security, wisdom, and a foretaste of heaven.</summary>
<keywords>Christian virtue, Holy Spirit, imitation of Christ, mortification, divine love, God's attributes, inner peace, spiritual discipline</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the publisher's preface from page iv. The opening fragment "ing" completes the word "raging" from the final sentence of the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>v</page-num>
->**The PUBLISHER to the READER.**<-

...raging flame of our passions. If we set each mental faculty to perform its specific role, not allowing one to interfere with the functions of another; and if we make our senses obey the mind, and then submit the mind to **GOD**—what more orderly or happy way of living <note>original: "oeconomy"; here meaning the management or internal organization of one's soul and life</note> could we achieve? 

This will bless us with eternal peace and adorn our soul like a glorious throne for the reception of our sovereign Lord. It will raise the soul from the gutter of sensual pleasures to a blessed communion <note>original: "intercourse"; in this context, spiritual fellowship or communication</note> with the Holy Trinity. It will make the soul prefer the glory and happiness arising from a Christian life over all the advantages of natural talent or worldly wealth <note>original: "nature and fortune"</note>. 

If you wish to know the authors of these *Discourses* <note>original: "Discourses"; formal writings or sermons on a specific subject</note>, it is enough to say that some were learned men, high-ranking church officials, and ministers from various Christian denominations. Some were of noble birth and served in the army; others lived a secluded, contemplative life; and some were uneducated and worked in the lowliest manual trades. They lived in different eras and were from different nations and languages, but all of them were assisted by the Holy Spirit of **GOD** to trace sin and vice back to their source, and

<summary>The publisher explains that by disciplining the mind and senses, one can achieve a peaceful, God-centered life that far outweighs worldly success. He introduces the authors of the book's essays, noting they come from diverse backgrounds—ranging from noblemen and scholars to uneducated tradesmen—all united by the Holy Spirit.</summary>

<page-num>vi</page-num>
<header>The PUBLISHER to the READER.</header>
<meta>bleed-through from reverse page visible</meta>

*...to show how to stop their veins and drain their springs <note>The author uses the metaphor of stopping or draining a water source to describe cutting off the flow of sin at its origin.</note> by properly using the means given to us by* **GOD** *for a holy resistance to our corrupt desires. It is not necessary—and perhaps not even advisable—to know more about these authors while there is such intense hostility <note>original: "heats"; meaning passionate arguments or conflicts</note> among the various factions of Christians. However, this work is sure to receive a kind and friendly welcome from everyone except those who have entirely abandoned themselves to desperate immorality and a stubborn mind. Such people cannot expect to find anything here to satisfy their corrupt nature, since our authors speak out openly against such behavior. Instead, they wrote their books for those who lead a spiritual life—a life that those focused on worldly pleasures are ignorant of.*

*It is a great shame that many well-meaning people—who strongly support the literal words of Christianity—are such enemies to its spirit that they lash out against all internal workings of the soul. They label these experiences as "Enthusiasm" <term>Enthusiasm</term>, "Fanaticism," and whatever else their resentment and dislike can suggest! All the mockery and insults directed at revealed religion by Deists <term>Deists</term> and Atheists are also used by*

<term>Enthusiasm</term> — In the 17th and 18th centuries, this was a negative term for people who claimed to receive direct, private revelations from God, which critics saw as irrational or delusional.
<term>Deists</term> — People who believe in a creator God based on reason and nature, but reject the "revealed" truths of the Bible or God's direct intervention in human affairs.

<meta>catchword: some</meta>

<summary>The publisher explains that the book is intended for those seeking a spiritual life and warns that it will be rejected by the immoral. He also critiques those who mock the inner, spiritual experiences of faith by labeling them as "enthusiasm" or "fanaticism."</summary>

<keywords>spirituality, internal operations, Enthusiasm, Fanaticism, Deists, Atheists, religious conflict, moral discipline</keywords>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>The PUBLISHER to the READER.</header>

*...lash out <note>original: "fome" (foam); meaning to froth at the mouth with rage or speak with great heat</note> against what they call Mysticism, treating it as the most insulting name they can devise. Meanwhile, there is nothing more sinister in Mysticism, or "Mysticity," than the fact that this harmless word simply represents the secret guidance of the Spirit of* **GOD**. *As the Apostle Paul has testified, the human spirit is naturally unable to understand divine matters. His authority should be final for those who accept him as a teacher of divine truths—one who taught with simplicity and godly sincerity, not with worldly <note>original: "carnal"</note> wisdom, but as* **GOD** *revealed those truths to him by His Spirit. Nor does "Enthusiasm" <term>Enthusiasm</term> (in its true and ancient sense) mean anything other than these same divine communications. The only difference is that people under its influence have sometimes experienced ecstasies <note>original: "extasies"</note> and raptures, just as the Prophets and Apostles did on extraordinary occasions. Do not let the high level of purity proposed by some of our authors frighten anyone, for we can do all things through* **CHRIST** *who strengthens us. These are the exact commands of our Savior and the practices of his first disciples: to reach heaven by way of the cross. Therefore, do not complain that there are too many difficulties in...*

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<term>Enthusiasm</term> — While modern readers see this as simple excitement, in the 17th and 18th centuries, it was a controversial term for "divine inspiration." Critics used it to mock people they believed were hallucinating or falsely claiming to hear from God.

<summary>The publisher defends the concepts of Mysticism and Enthusiasm, arguing they are not dangerous "fanaticism" but rather the necessary guidance of the Holy Spirit as described by the Apostle Paul. He encourages readers to pursue the high standards of purity found in the book, framing the spiritual struggle as the "way of the cross."</summary>
<keywords>Mysticism, Enthusiasm, Spirit of God, Apostle Paul, divine revelation, spiritual purity, way of the cross, religious defense</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the publisher’s argument that the rigorous spiritual life is both natural and superior to ancient philosophy. It completes the sentence from the previous page: "Therefore, do not complain that there are too many difficulties in..."</meta>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
<header>The Publisher to the Reader.</header>

...the exercise of **CHRIST'S** precepts, which bring such an infinite reward with them. They are not contrary even to nature; for all the philosophers were aware that mortification <term>mortification</term> was useful for the rooting out of vice. They aimed at the perfection of which they were capable by refining their nature with abstinence, renouncing pleasures, correcting their passions and senses, and finally by focusing their minds on the contemplation of the highest and most worthy objects—striving (as one of them has said) to exalt themselves above a purely rational life and attain a kind of god-like contemplation. 

For unless men study to correct themselves in this way, all their sacrifices, prayers, and worship will be in vain. If pagans <note>original: "Heathens"</note> spoke and acted so well by the light of reason alone, how much higher might the heavenly fire of grace and love elevate our natures and make us like **GOD**, who is love? But instead of that grace influencing our actions, we see the fire of anger, hatred, and envy pushing men toward strife, conflict, and murder; and the flames of lust breaking out into lewdness and obscenity. Can men hope to find the way to hea- <note>The text likely continues "heaven" on the following page.</note>

<term>mortification</term> — The practice of self-discipline and abstaining from physical pleasures or comforts in order to overcome sin and strengthen the spirit.

<summary>The publisher argues that spiritual discipline is consistent with human nature, noting that even ancient philosophers practiced self-denial to reach a higher state of mind. He contrasts the "heavenly fire" of divine love with the destructive "flames" of human passions like anger and lust.</summary>

<keywords>philosophers, mortification, contemplation, grace, reason, passions, vice, morality</keywords>

<page-num>ix</page-num>

->### The PUBLISHER to the READER.<-

...heaven by such dull torches as these, or be admitted into the choir of seraphim <note>original: "seraphims"; the highest order of angels, associated with light and purity</note>—spirits inflamed with the pure love of **GOD**—or cherubim <note>original: "cherubims"</note> who shine with innate and fundamental light? Surely not; for the fire of love and self-discipline <term>mortification</term> which should refine them from their impurities <note>original: "dross"</note> and corruption here, shall after this life kindle the anger of **GOD**. It will raise a terrifying fire <note>original: "direful conflagration"</note> against them and convert their fiery passions into unquenchable flames, which shall torment them for ages everlasting. In contrast, the sincere love of **GOD** would dispel these thick mists that darken the soul and prove to be that vital heat that preserves it active, lively, and glorious for all eternity.

**GOD**, through various ways and instruments, offers His saving <note>original: "salutary"</note> truth to humanity. He does this so that, according to the different temperaments <note>original: "constitutions"</note> of their minds, everyone may find what is most agreeable to his own taste and condition, in order to prepare and entice him to seek and aspire after divine love. And whoever will read these *Discourses* <term>Discourses</term> with a free and unbiased mind, and with a sincere intention to do the will of **GOD**,

<meta>catchword: according</meta>

<term>mortification</term> — The practice of self-denial and spiritual discipline to overcome worldly desires.

<term>Discourses</term> — A formal and orderly speech or writing on a specific subject, in this case, a series of religious or philosophical treatises.

<summary>The publisher explains that if the soul is not purified by divine love on earth, its passions will be turned into eternal punishment. He asserts that God provides spiritual truths in various forms to appeal to different human natures and lead them toward salvation.</summary>

<keywords>divine love, Seraphim, Cherubim, mortification, judgment, salvation, spiritual temperament</keywords>

<term>accessories</term> — Secondary or non-essential details that accompany the core truths (essentials) of a religious or philosophical matter.

<meta>This page begins a preface addressed to the reader. It follows the title page and sets the stage for a discussion on the state of contemporary Christianity. The catchword "relinquish" indicates the text continues on the next page with that word.</meta>

# THE PUBLISHER TO THE READER.

The wisdom that comes from above is first pure, then peaceable. *It is all too obvious how far the Christian world is today from these divine qualities. People have so transformed Christian doctrine with their interpretations <note>original: "glosses"; referring here to biased explanations or commentaries added to a text</note> that it is now hardly to be found among them in its original truth and simplicity. The doctrine of Jesus Christ, as it was delivered to us by his Apostles, is no longer considered the standard of Christianity. Instead, people look to the catechisms, the creeds, and the church laws <note>original: "canons"</note> of the different denominations <note>original: "sects"</note> within the Christian world <note>original: "Christendom"</note>. In defense of which, men—*

<summary>The publisher critiques the state of 17th-century Christianity, arguing that the simple teachings of Jesus and the Apostles have been replaced by the complex creeds and laws of various religious factions.</summary>
<keywords>Christian doctrine, Apostles, catechisms, creeds, church canons, Christendom, sectarianism, religious reform</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the corruption of Christian practice, specifically how sectarianism and pride have replaced the core values of love for God and neighbor.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
# The Publisher to the Reader.

...relinquish and reject the true spirit and essence of Christianity—the love of **GOD** and their neighbors—and create for themselves doctrines that flatter their corrupt nature and foster their pride and envy. In some nations, it has been a primary obsession for different families and tribes to maintain feuds and enmities among themselves, pursuing them to the furthest extremes in the mutual destruction of one another. Such a bloody and irreconcilable disposition, while rightly considered barbarous in those people, is nevertheless regarded as a gracious and holy zeal <note>original: "zeal"; here meaning an intense, often narrow-minded religious fervor</note> within the various tribes and parties of Christians. Each group competes so earnestly and disputes with such heat and intensity to maintain their own sect and set of opinions in opposition to all others. 

Furthermore, the majority of the various spiritual leaders and guides are so far from doing their duty to restrain these barbarities that they will not even hear of any principles or proposals that might lead in that direction. Instead, if anything is offered that might help to soften <note>original: "sweeten"; to make more pleasant or reconciled</note> the minds of people toward one another, these leaders make it their business to treat the proposers as common enemies. They portray both the individuals and their ideas in such a way as to lead the public to abhor them.

<summary>The author critiques the sectarianism of his time, arguing that religious leaders often encourage division and hostility rather than the core Christian values of love and peace.</summary>
<keywords>sectarianism, religious conflict, church leaders, Christian love, zeal, intolerance, 17th-century religion</keywords>

<page-num>4</page-num>

# The Publisher to the Reader.

<margin>April 5</margin>

*The prejudices that false representations of religion cause to its true interests, and to the souls of men, are innumerable. How harmful they are to societies and nations <note>original: "commonwealths"</note> is clearly evidenced by both the present and former ages. Therefore, we are all concerned—both as human beings and as Christians—to recognize in this our day the things that contribute to our peace, before they are hidden from our eyes.* <note>This is a reference to the biblical verse Luke 19:42: "If you had known... the things which belong unto your peace! but now they are hidden from your eyes."</note>

*Most of the following discourse was originally intended to instill in the minds of youth trained in theological studies <note>the formal study of religious faith, practice, and experience</note> such accurate and balanced sentiments of religion in all its parts. The goal was to increase their concern for the things in religion that are absolutely necessary and essential, and to protect them from a blind, uncontrollable, and furious zeal <note>original: "zeal"; an intense, often narrow-minded religious fervor</note> for those other matters that are either merely incidental <note>original: "circumstantial"</note> or at least not necessary. Nevertheless, these minor matters are now made to be the foundation <note>original: "the pillar and ground"</note> of most, if not all, of the unfortunate and inexplicable conflicts that currently exist throughout the Christian world.*

*That this discourse may, by the blessing of God, help lead people to the true knowledge and love of God in Jesus Christ, and toward mutual peace and charity <note>original: "charity"; in this context, brotherly love and kindness toward others</note> with one another, is the earnest prayer of*

ALTHOUGH all the parts of the Christian religion agree in this—that they are revealed and commanded by GOD, and also that they are directed toward the glory of GOD and the salvation of mankind—yet they are not all of the same weight and importance. When weighed in the balance of a sound judgment, some of them are found to be more weighty than others. The Prophets tell us that *mercy, the knowledge of God, and obedience to his commands* are more acceptable to him than *sacrifices and burnt offerings* (1 Samuel 15:22; Hosea 6:6). Our blessed Lord also calls *judgment, mercy, and truth*, when compared with *tithes of mint, cumin, and anise*, the weightier matters of the law <note>original: τὰ βαρύτερα τοῦ νόμου</note>, the more weighty things

# The true and solid Grounds

8

...matters of the law (Matthew 23:23). From this consideration arises a distinction or division of that skill, knowledge, and understanding which deals with religion—commonly called theology or divinity—into "absolute" and "respective" <note>In this context, "respective" means "relative" or "comparative," referring to the study of how different religious truths relate to one another in importance.</note>. Absolute theology, or the knowledge of religion I call absolute, considers its subject matter only as it has been revealed, commanded, or established by <term>God</term>. Its purpose is to identify those things proposed to us in the Scriptures to be believed or practiced, and to distinguish them from all other things. 

On the other hand, respective theology—or the right knowledge of religion—weighs the importance of these matters in the mind. It observes the order, relationship, and connection of things belonging to religion, whether they are points of doctrine, moral precepts, or sacred rites. It teaches how to distinguish and differentiate between the secondary accessories of religion and its primary principles—the circumstantial details versus the essential substance, and the means versus their intended ends.

Regarding the *first* point, we must consider that the essence of religion—or the duty which man owes to God, or which God requires of man in any state, whether that of integrity and innocence, restoration after the fall, or ultimate happiness <note>original: "consummated felicity"</note>—consists solely in the **LOVE OF GOD**. Because man is a creature made up of a soul (a spiritual substance) and a material body, <term>God</term> created this visible and physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> world. He then made man lord over it. He did this so that just as the soul had <term>God</term>—the infinite good—perfectly suited to its vast capacity, the body also would not be without suitable and delightful objects. 

The history of the world's creation agrees perfectly with this design of <term>God</term>. We read that this whole visible world was first created and finished like a magnificent palace, richly provided with all kinds of furnishings. After this was done, man—who was designed to be its lord and master—was created and granted dominion over it.

For such great favors, so generously given <note>original: "liberally dispensed"</note> to humanity, <term>God</term> required no other testimony of a grateful mind but <term>love</term>. And so that his love might be noble and free, generous and unlimited—not compelled, forced, or restricted—it was the will of <term>God</term> that man should be at his own disposal. Therefore, he endowed him with a free power to determine how to use his own faculties and toward which objects to direct them; he was free to cling <note>original: "adhering"</note> to <term>God</term> through love, or to depart from him. Provided he still continued to love <term>God</term> and to place his delight in him alone, nothing was forbidden to him. All things were lawful to him, and he was the master of a most free power (not only in a physical but also in a moral sense) to use all his faculties and the creatures subject to him as he pleased, without causing any offense to <term>God</term>.

of religion began to be expanded. Some of these were useful and others were necessary for recovering the *love of God* that was lost, and for bringing humanity—which had gone astray—back onto the path of duty.

From what has already been said, this brief and general idea of the doctrine of Christian Theology emerges: namely, in the state of *nature's integrity* <term>integrity: the original state of moral wholeness and perfection before sin</term> and innocence, the *love of God* made up the whole of religion; nor was there any other duty required of man, but *to love the Lord his God with all his heart*. Again, in the state of *depraved nature* <term>depraved nature: the human condition corrupted by sin after the Fall</term>—and considering how it is to be repaired and renewed—the same *love of God* still forms the first, the chief, and the most essential <note>original: "capital"</note> part of religion. This love alone is desired for its own sake, and it is for the sake of this love that all the other things belonging to religion are commanded. So it is that the love of GOD, being compared with the remaining parts of religion, is the end <note>original: "end" here means the ultimate goal or purpose</note> to which they all refer, and to which they lead as so many ways and means.

To provide a more detailed and clear explanation of this doctrine of Christian Theology, which has been briefly summarized, we will next show how and in what order all the other commands and practices of religion relate to charity—or the *love of God*—and serve to reignite that heavenly flame in the hearts of people.

First, then, since humanity had unworthily broken the bond <term>original: "league," referring to a formal agreement or covenant</term> of friendship into which God had graciously <term>original: "vouchsafed"</term> admitted them, and by shifting the love and affection due only to God onto created things, had brought God’s displeasure upon themselves and exposed themselves to eternal death; it was impossible for humanity to be restored to their former state without the grace and mercy of God pardoning their offense. This pardon was offered on these terms (and no easier terms could be desired or given): namely, that people should return to their duty—which is the *love of God*—and that to achieve this, God would provide the help necessary for that purpose.

To obtain this mercy, the intercession of a Mediator was necessary—one who could secure this through his own merit and favor with God. I shall not make it my task here

Therefore, some means must necessarily be prescribed and used for this purpose. Through these means, as if by steps, we may gradually ascend to the perfect *love of God*, which is seated, as it were, upon the high throne of religion. <term>God</term> has prescribed such means in his word, and by doing so, he has adopted them into the family of religion.

It is clear and evident in itself that, in returning to the *love of God*, we must take a course completely opposite to the one by which humanity departed from it. Since man lost the love of <term>God</term>—not by enjoying or taking pleasure in created things <note>original: "creatures," referring to anything in the material or natural world created by God</note> (both of which he could lawfully do), but by binding his heart to them through love—it follows that if we want this divine fire to de- <meta>catchword: scer [descend]</meta>

When the mind is properly affected with a sorrowful awareness of sin and its own misery, it first groans under that burden and aspires to be delivered from it. The methods used to extinguish the love of created things <note>original: "the creatures"; referring to worldly attachments or physical beings as opposed to the Creator</note> in human hearts are the same ones that Christ and his apostles so frequently urge upon us in the Holy Scriptures, when they command us to practice *repentance, self-denial, renouncing the world, crucifying the flesh, taking up the cross, and putting off the old man.*

Furthermore, just as it is only the gracious and immediate presence of God <note>original: "the Deity"</note>, filling the soul with light and joy, that can produce the true love of God—which is the perfection of all religion—so the other means of salvation spring from a higher and far more noble principle than our own nature. Because human nature, corrupted <note>original: "depraved"</note> as it is by sin, is blind and seeks only itself and its own advantage in all things, always and everywhere; and because the commands of religion we are discussing are most unpleasant and even hateful to that nature, it is clear that we cannot expect to comply with them without the assistance of divine grace. Having established the general concept of this theology, along with its more distinct and detailed explanation, I now come to the many important propositions, rules, and conclusions that can be drawn from this doctrine. Indeed, the use of this pure and peaceable theology (which I have already explained) is so varied and extensive—both in private life and for the proper organization of all Christian religious societies—and it offers so many useful rules and instructions for every way of life, that it would be difficult to list everything that could be derived from it through serious and attentive meditation. —sary to be observed by all who wish to be saved, is not so that he might exercise his authority over people or be avenged on them. Instead, it is because there is no other safe and secure way of raising them to a true and lasting happiness besides the path that the teachings <note>original: "precepts"</note> of religion prescribe. 

God might indeed, by virtue of his omnipotence <term>all-powerful nature</term>, have restored humanity—who had fallen into sin and misery—to their original holy and happy state without the intervention of a healing repentance <note>original: "medicinal penitence"</note>. However, recent experience and the notable example of instability and inconstancy shown by *Adam* clearly reveal that nothing could be expected from such a method. If *Adam* had escaped such a great danger so easily, he would likely have been even more bold <note>original: "presumptuous"</note> to offend again. Or, even if his own experience had made him more cautious and wary, his children might still have easily fallen back <note>original: "elapsed"</note> into his sin and misery. 

Therefore, if this method had been used to recover fallen humanity, it is likely that none of them would have been saved by it. For this reason, it was more consistent with both the wisdom and goodness of God to grant fallen humanity the means of returning to their original state of happiness...

<summary>The author argues that God requires repentance not to show off his power, but because it is the only effective way to ensure humanity does not repeat Adam's fall. He suggests that a simple, unearned restoration would have left humans prone to sinning again.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Fall of Man, repentance, divine wisdom, salvation, omnipotence, human nature</keywords>

For just as those who come into great fortunes—either by inheriting them from their parents or through the generosity <note>original: "bounty"</note> of their prince—are more likely to squander their wealth than others who have earned riches through their own industry and labor after feeling the painful <note>original: "pinching"</note> difficulties of want and poverty; so there is no doubt that those who have struggled out of the deep gulf of sin and misery with great difficulty, and have reached happiness through the rough, uneasy, and narrow path of penitence, will prove much more constant in the love of their Creator. They will be more firm and resolute in enduring and resisting <note>original: "repelling"</note> the assaults of temptation than *Adam* was, who was created in a state of bliss <note>original: "felicity"</note>, or than his descendants would have been if that happiness had been passed down to them by inheritance <note>original: "succession"</note> from *Adam*.

I answer: A permanent <term>original: "confirmed"</term> state of grace and holiness is both the result and the reward of a previously tested faithfulness and stability in the love of God. Similarly, it would not have been given to *Adam*—even if he had not sinned—until after a sufficient <note>original: "competent"</note> time of trial spent faithfully in the love of God. Finally, it is not possible for a rational creature to reach a state of complete perfection by any other way than what God has appointed without violating its own freedom <note>original: "liberty"</note>, which God insists must be preserved safe and untouched <note>original: "inviolate"</note>.

For such is the nature of freedom that, without harm or damage to itself, it cannot allow any of the faculties under its guidance—especially the will—to be dictated by any principle other than itself. It must be the one to choose one suitable object over another, or to act in one way rather than another. Nevertheless, freedom will allow itself to be fixed and bound, either to good or evil, by cords of its own making. These cords are the habits formed <note>original: "contracted"</note> by long and continued practice of acts of love and desire...

fire, pleasure <note>original: "complacency"; in this context, it refers to a state of being pleased or satisfied with something</note>, enjoyment, and the like, toward the same objects. Through this, a man finds himself pushed forward toward the love, desire, and enjoyment of those objects, and pulled back when he tries to separate and withdraw his affections from them. This has given rise to that common <note>original: "vulgar"; meaning widely known or popular at the time</note> maxim, "Custom is a second nature."

This is plain and undeniable in the case of evil habits, which are so common and ordinary. <page-num>37</page-num>

# of CHRISTIAN THEOLOGY.

*...can you also do good, who are accustomed to doing evil?* (Jeremiah 13:23).

There is no less power and effectiveness in good habits, which are developed through a long course of good actions performed by the power of the Holy Spirit. Through these habits, the "old man" <note>original: "old man"; a biblical term referring to the unregenerate, sinful nature of humanity</note> is subdued and put to death <term>original: "mortified"; the spiritual practice of subduing one's worldly or sinful desires</term>, while the "new man" <note>original: "new man"; the regenerate person who has been spiritually reborn</note> is brought to life <note>original: "quickned"</note> and matures by degrees, until in the end he becomes a perfect <note>In this context, "perfect" likely refers to spiritual maturity or completeness rather than absolute sinlessness</note> man. For when any person has reached this stage, he becomes ready, eager, and inclined to do good, while remaining slow, hesitant, and resistant to evil. As St. *John* instructs us: *Whoever is born of God does not commit sin; for the seed of God remains in him, and he cannot sin, because he is born of God* (1 John 3:9).

After these good or evil habits have been further developed by God—that is, after He has strengthened and established the good through renewed measures and impartings <note>original: "communications"</note> of His grace, and after He has repeatedly tried to reclaim those who are evil, all to no purpose; when He resolves that the graces of His Spirit shall no longer be subjected <note>original: "prostituted"</note> to the mockery and contempt of those who are deeply rooted in sin; and when He rewards the godly for the good use they have made of their freedom <note>original: "liberty"; referring to the human will's capacity to choose</note>,

with <meta>catchword: with</meta>

From this, it appears that rational, free creatures can neither be established in a state of grace nor hardened into a state of sin without their liberty being violated, except through the free use and exercise of that liberty in doing good or evil.

[...means of] salvation, both necessary, sure, and infallible; others are useful, but not certain; and others may be means of salvation, but are neither certain nor necessary in any particular form or mode. It is evident that the precepts and institutions of this third class do by no means belong to the category <note>original: "tribe"</note> of the fundamentals of religion.

Moreover, because a "fundamental" of religion, properly so called, is something which must be known for salvation directly and in itself—and only the precepts and institutions of the first class are of this nature—it appears that the entire collection <note>original: "family"</note> of the fundamentals of religion is contained within the limits of the first class. However, because humans cannot come to the certain knowledge of these fundamentals through any other outward means as effectively as through the Scriptures, knowing the Scriptures and acknowledging them as a sure rule of faith may be called a secondary principle of religion.

[reli-]gion, and that is according to the opinion and judgment of the person in error; or those that cannot exist at the same time and in the same mind as a belief in the fundamental principles of religion: these, and these only, are to be looked upon as destructive and damnable. For the Christian religion is like a bridge, by which alone people can go from this valley of miseries to the blessed regions of light and peace. The fundamental principles of religion are like so many arches which, joined and united together, make up this bridge. Therefore, those errors that overturn any of the fundamental principles of religion essentially cut an arch out of the bridge; by this, a breach is made in it, and the passage by it to heaven is obstructed.

Furthermore, those errors are to be avoided as dangerous if their tendency is to make people neglectful <note>original: "remiss"</note> and lazy in the business of their salvation. From this, in the fifth place, we may draw rules and principles to guide not only private Christians in deciding how far they should engage in or refuse external communion <term>external communion: participating in public worship, sacraments, or formal membership with a church body</term> with the different sects and societies of Christians—into which the outward unity of Christian societies is miserably divided and torn apart—but also to guide those who are appointed by each society to ensure that the rules and regulations established for good order are observed by their respective members. 

Since the purpose and goal of Christian societies is that, through their help, every private Christian may be advanced in a course of piety <note>original: "piety," meaning religious devotion and moral conduct</note> and assisted in working out their salvation, it is plain, first: That a Christian who takes the work of his salvation seriously ought to join and associate himself with those Christian societies where he believes the best helps to piety are to be found.

In this corollary <term>corollary: a logical conclusion that follows naturally from an argument already proven</term>, we may see, as if in a mirror <note>original: "as in a glass"</note>, the deplorable state of the Christian world today. In this world, almost every sect of Christians—or at least their ruling members—requires the belief and profession of their own peculiar and specific doctrines as a necessary condition for communion. Some of these doctrines are obscure, others are doubtful and uncertain, and others are simply false. By doing this, they sow the seeds of discord, division, and hatred among Christians, instead of the unity, love, and harmony <note>original: "concord"</note> that our Savior so earnestly recommended to his disciples. Nor is this all; they also do everything in their power to force others—through fines <note>original: "mulcts"</note>, banishments, and physical punishments—to embrace their communion and fulfill its conditions. By doing so, instead of making people into Christians, they only make them hypocrites.

FROM all that has been said, the significant use and necessity of this "pure and peaceable theology" is abundantly clear. For anyone who does not understand the various ways the different parts of religion relate to one another (which is what this theology teaches) will inevitably stumble, fall, and wander off the path to eternal life. For such a person will likely face one of two problems:

*First*, he will consider all the parts of religion as equal and standing on the same level. Consequently, he will apply himself to them indifferently, without any sense of order or choice. He will practice one duty now and another later, simply as his inclinations or circumstances prompt him. He is not unlike an unskilled farmer <note>original: "husbandman"</note> who takes great pains in sowing, digging, tilling, and harrowing his ground, but does so without any regard for the proper order or season that ought to be observed. <header>The true and solid Grounds</header>

the greater part of Christians are guilty, who put a greater value upon a right belief and persuasion touching things less necessary—such as are almost all the distinguishing doctrines of every sect of Christians, together with some religious rites and particular rules and forms of discipline and government, much controverted <note>original: "controverted"; meaning highly debated or disputed</note> in these times; concerning which a man may think and act thus or otherwise, provided he act not against his conscience, without prejudice to his salvation—than they do upon holiness of heart and life, without which no man shall see <term>God</term>, *Hebrews* 12:14. They will slight and neglect the secondary, ministering, and subservient <note>original: "subservient"; here meaning supportive or useful for a higher purpose</note> parts and duties of religion, and seek to reach those of the highest rank and degree without them. They meet with no better success than if a man should endeavor to get to the top of a high tower without the help of the stairs that lead to it, by throwing out his arms, straining his body, and standing on tip-toe. 

And this fault is all too common among Christians, who imagine they can attain the internal virtues of contrition, humility, contempt for the world, self-denial, the love of <term>God</term>, and so forth, by meditation and thinking alone. They do this without practicing the external good works that prepare the way and lead to those virtues, and without avoiding the allurements and temptations of the opposite vices—such as riches, honors, pleasures, and the familiarity and friendship of worldly-minded men. In this, they act with as little reason as someone who, being shut up in a dark prison or plunged in snow up to his chin, expects to be enlightened and warmed by the mere force of his imagination, without coming out into the bright and warm sun.

They see the other duties and virtues belonging to the Christian religion as the means appointed for reaching that goal. Accordingly, they make this love their primary aim and focus, and they value and practice all other duties only because of how they serve <note>original: "subserviency"</note> this primary objective. 

Such a person reads the Scriptures, listens to sermons, receives the sacrament, and performs other supporting duties of religion, not so that they may find satisfaction in the acts themselves, but so that through these means they may be stirred up and helped forward toward humility <note>original: "contempt of himself"; in this context, it refers to self-denial and the rejection of pride rather than self-hatred</note> and a disregard for worldly distractions. Their aim is to grow in the love of GOD and of their neighbor for God's sake, knowing that all the labor spent on religious practice is lost if it does not contribute to this end.

# THE
# PREFACE.

I have something specific to say about this work. Its contents are so relevant to the current age that there will hardly be a person who reads it who will not find themselves interested in the topics discussed. The reader must judge whether the approach I have taken is more satisfactory than what is usually provided.

Christ is the power of God, through which the Holy Spirit renews the image of God in humanity and restores us to that original <term>primitive: original or ancient</term> dignity and perfection from which we fell through sin. This allows a person, after their regeneration <note>original: "regeneration"; the theological process of spiritual rebirth or being "born again"</note> and becoming a "new creature," to enjoy a blessed and glorious immortality. Because of this, the value or harm of writings, doctrines, and actions should be measured by how much they truly help or hinder this great goal. Since there are some extremely dangerous errors that people easily embrace, I will mention a few that seem to have the most harmful influence in this country <note>original: "this island"; referring to Great Britain</note>.

The first I shall mention is that fundamental and primary error of denying original...

<meta>This page continues the author's list of dangerous theological errors, specifically addressing the denial of original sin and the controversial doctrine of absolute predestination.</meta>

-># THE PREFACE.<-

...original corruption of human nature, or the idea that we inherit from our parents a corrupt and depraved nature that has completely declined from what it was when it first came from God. On the other hand, some argue that human nature is much the same now as it was when first created, and that the powers and faculties of the mind are also the same. They claim that sin has made very little change in humanity and that people remain much the same as they were before the fall of Adam, possessing the same faculties, the same strength, and the same powers. These thinkers suggest it is a mere fantasy to imagine that human beings are now born so weak, so corrupt, and so dead in sin that only the internal grace and work of the Spirit of God can restore them; they believe that reason is sufficient to keep a person in a state of integrity before God without the help of any internal, active grace poured directly into the soul by the Spirit of God.

Some say that all human actions, both good and evil, are predetermined by God from all eternity. Indeed, they argue that He produces and determines all the actions of His creatures within time, but in such a way that the human will is not forced. They believe that a person must inevitably sin by their own choice, so that all the fault and blame lies solely on them. Others, with good reason, consider this a horrible blasphemy <note>original: "blasphemy"; here meaning a teaching that dishonors the character or nature of God</note> that exceeds the malice...

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of extreme predestination, arguing that if God preordained every detail of a sinful act, He would be the author of sin. It asserts that human free will is a necessary condition for the existence of sin and moral responsibility.</meta>

-># THE PREFACE.<-

...and the wickedness of devils and the worst of men, who never reached such a height of irreverence as to say that God disposes even good people to sin and ordains every concurring circumstance so that they may fall. Some even claim God performs within a person the very act that constitutes sin, yet does so with such skill and artifice <note>original: "dextrously and artificially"; meaning with cleverness and intentional design</note> that He—the primary cause—remains innocent, while the person alone is held guilty.

Humanity must necessarily have been endowed with free will—a power of self-determination—at least before sin was committed; otherwise, there could have been no sin at all. If everything had been determined by God—specifically the goal a person was to aim for, the means to reach it, every circumstance, the innermost principle of action, and even the act itself—then a person could not be said to sin by pursuing a goal through means and circumstances already determined by God. Or, if there were evil in such an act, God would be the cause of it, which is a sacrilegious <note>original: "impious"</note> thought. Since humanity actually did commit evil, we must have had liberty and free will; and since people continue to do evil, we must still possess that same free will. The actions and circumstances resulting from this free will cannot be determined by God for the very same reason: if they were, there would be no evil in them, or God Himself would be the author of it.

<summary>The author argues that if God determined all human actions, He would be the source of sin; therefore, human free will is essential for moral accountability.</summary>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the necessity of free will, asserting that it is an inherent part of human consciousness and the basis for all moral and divine relationships.</meta>

-># THE PREFACE.<-

*Every person is conscious of this free will; and if anyone says otherwise, their own heart and actions must prove them a liar <note>original: "give him the lie"</note>. All human dealings with one another, and God's dealings with humanity, assume this principle of freedom in man. By this principle, a person may determine their own path as they please; it is a core element in humanity upon which nothing—except one's own choice—imposes a necessity to act.*

*This power is granted to man because his nature has a divine origin; he is God's offspring, and he was made in the image of God. This is because God wanted the gift of relationship to be mutual between Himself and humanity. Just as He united Himself to humanity freely, out of pure choice and in a most generous manner, He also wanted humanity to cling to God out of choice—freely and generously, without any coercion or external influence <note>original: "foreign determination"</note>. He desired to be loved by humanity with a true, noble, sincere, and free love.*

*Some say that God has given man free will regarding civil and worldly matters, but not regarding divine and spiritual things. And yet, free will is the most divine of all our advantages; therefore, it must apply to spiritual things just as much as it does to temporal <term>temporal</term> <note>original: "temporal"; meaning worldly or related to time, as opposed to eternal</note> things.*

*A person's free will is not the cause of the faculties <note>original: "faculties"; meaning natural mental or spiritual powers</note> they possess, nor is it the reason those faculties naturally seek after light and love, nor why they are active...*

This doctrine of human free will defends <note>original: "vindicates"</note> divine justice and goodness. In contrast, the opposing doctrine <note>likely referring to absolute predestination or determinism</note> blasphemously makes God the author of evil by suggesting He inevitably <note>original: "fatally"</note> determines the human will in all things. This gives sinful people an excuse for all their sins, as if they were forced into them by the will of God; it also encourages people to remain in their dull laziness <note>original: "stupid sloth"</note> and spiritual slumber.

The doctrine of human free will is not inconsistent with the doctrine of divine grace; instead, they mutually strengthen one another. All that the divine Being has established as a person's duty is this: that since the soul of man is free and has it in its power to choose as it will—to turn to this or that; to admit this or the other; or not to admit it; and to do so more or less as it pleases—the free will of man must turn its desires away from everything that is not God. It must comply with the good impulses <note>original: "motions"</note> that God works in the mind by His grace, and bring the desires of the heart and offer them—and all that belongs to them—entirely to God. But if a person does not do this, God will never compensate for this failure and stubbornness <note>original: "defect and obstinacy"</note>. This is man's duty and his essential work, which he cannot neglect without ruining himself for all eternity. There is nothing more true and nothing more important in the world than this. This is the core <note>original: "kernel"</note> and the very essence <note>original: "quintessence"</note> of true religion and the genuine worship that God requires from humanity, which the entire sequence of divine providence and divine revelation proclaims and speaks clearly.

There is a real, living, certain, enlightening <note>original: "luminous"</note>, active <note>original: "operative"</note>, satisfying, and solid knowledge of things; and there is a theoretical <note>original: "speculative"</note>, dry, and barren...

-># THE PREFACE.<-

and superficial knowledge of God and of divine things. This occurs when those who have had some degree of the living knowledge of God and of divine matters—and whose moral reason and faculties have been enlightened with divine ideas—suddenly turn away from their dependence upon God. Instead, they give themselves over to the speculations of their own reason and the construction of their own ideas; by comparing these ideas, they draw their own inferences and conclusions about divine things.

Alternatively, it happens when those who were never so enlightened—and have only received that "manifestation of the Spirit" given to everyone for their benefit <note>original: "to profit withal"</note>—devote themselves to following the activities of their own reason. When such people read the writings of those who were divinely inspired to encourage <note>original: "excite"</note> men to turn their souls toward God, they do not obey the directions for obtaining the true light. Instead, they immediately set about using their blind minds to form their own ideas of divine truths. In this, they differ as much from one another and from the truth as blind men do when debating the nature of light and colors. They pass their time in empty distractions <note>original: "vain amusements"</note>, either by comparing what others have said or debating the sense in which certain words should be understood. <meta>This page begins a new section exploring the causes of human ignorance and the failure of earthly systems like philosophy or sectarian theology to resolve social and spiritual divisions. It follows the introduction's claim that reason alone cannot reach divine truth.</meta>

# Of NATURAL REASON
## And DIVINE FAITH.

Nothing, surely, can be more deplorable than to see and observe the ignorance, the divisions, and the distractions of mankind. Nothing can be a matter of greater concern to all sober, serious, and good men than to see how universally the world is governed and swayed in its judgments and opinions by the most selfish motives, such as honor, self-interest <note>original: "interest"</note>, passion, prejudice, and so forth <note>original: "&c."</note>. This is what everyone sees and observes, and what all honest and good men justly complain of and very sincerely lament. But at the same time <note>original: "withal"</note>, it is a problem for which few—very few—have so far been able to find and prescribe any sovereign and specific remedy.

Some have turned to the help <note>original: "succours"</note> of philosophy; others have applied themselves to the study of morality. But among Christians, the most approved method has been to adopt <note>original: "espouse"</note> this or that system of theology <note>original: "divinity"</note>. But, alas! How improper and unsuccessful any or all of these methods have been in healing our divisions and bringing us back to the way of truth, which can only be one;

<meta>catchword: one;</meta>

<summary>The author laments the deep divisions and selfish motives that govern human judgment, noting that philosophy, morality, and sectarian theology have all failed to restore mankind to the single path of truth.</summary>
<keywords>ignorance, human division, self-interest, philosophy, morality, theology, nature of truth</keywords>

# Of NATURAL REASON
## And DIVINE FAITH.

Nothing, surely, can be more deplorable than to see and observe the ignorance, the divisions, and the distractions of mankind. Nothing can be a matter of greater concern to all sober, serious, and good people than to see how universally the world is governed and swayed in its judgments and opinions by the most selfish motives, such as honor, self-interest, passion, and prejudice. This is what everyone sees and observes, and what all honest and good people justly complain of and very sincerely lament. But, at the same time, it is something against which few—very few—have so far been able to find or prescribe any sovereign and specific remedy.

Some have had recourse to the help of philosophy; others have applied themselves to the study of morality. But among Christians, the most approved method has been to adopt this or that system of divinity <note>original: "divinity"; here referring to a specific system of theology or religious doctrine</note>. But, alas! How improper and unsuccessful any, or all, of these methods have been to heal our divisions or to bring us back into the way of truth, which can be only one.

# Of NATURAL REASON
## And DIVINE FAITH.

Nothing, surely, can be more deplorable than to see and observe the ignorance, the divisions, and the distractions of mankind. Nothing can be a matter of greater concern to all sober, serious, and good people than to see how universally the world is governed and swayed in its judgments and opinions by the most selfish motives, such as honor, interest, passion, and prejudice. This is what everyone sees and observes, and what all honest and good people justly complain of and very sincerely lament. Some have turned for help to the assistance of philosophy; others have applied themselves to the study of morality. But among Christians, the most approved method has been to adopt this or that system of divinity <note>original: "divinity"; here referring to a specific school of theology or religious doctrine</note>. ...only one. That these systems have failed to unite mankind in judgment, heart, and affection is all too evident. The many unhappy and still-prevailing divisions and distractions—not only in the world in general, but even within Christendom in particular—provide all the proof we need!

Since this is clearly the case, the reader may be inclined to ask: Where do all these evils come from? Why is it that people embrace and hold such confused, unintelligible, and inconsistent notions and opinions? Why is it that instead of sticking to the truth—which is clear, uniform, and always consistent with itself—we are so bewildered by ignorance and error? Why have philosophy, morality, and even religion itself become so weak, confused, and obscure that they can neither influence and govern our lives and actions, nor enlighten and unite our hearts and feelings <note>original: "affections"; in this context, it refers to the emotional and spiritual inclinations of the soul</note>?

I readily admit that these are very appropriate and significant <note>original: "material"; here meaning substantial or important to the argument</note> questions that deserve every possible consideration. Therefore, in order to provide the clear and satisfactory answers they deserve, I believe it is necessary to begin by way of a preliminary explanation...

<meta>This page continues the author's introduction. Following his critique of existing systems of thought, he now transitions to establishing his own methodology, focusing on the four pillars of human understanding: reason, philosophy, morality, and religion.</meta>

...of introduction, some general but brief remarks upon the four most important <note>original: "material"</note> points that can possibly come within the scope of human knowledge: namely, reason, philosophy, morality, and religion.

It would be an endless and useless task to examine <note>original: "canvas"</note> all the writings and opinions of ancient and modern authors on these subjects. I will therefore take a shorter path and apply myself to a more infallible guide. Instead of wandering into the vast fields of academic learning and philosophy, I will stay close to the common path of sense and experience. Rather than being influenced one way or another by the uncertain and contradictory judgments of moralists and theologians <note>original: "divines"</note>, I will appeal to the hearts and consciences of people, and to the clear and direct words of Scripture, to demonstrate the truth of what I have to say.

1. Every person is, and cannot help but be, conscious within themselves that there is something in them that perceives, thinks, reflects, and moves <note>original: "actuates"</note> their entire body.

2. Whatever this thing may be that is gifted <note>original: "endued"</note> with those various and remarkable powers and faculties, it must be distinct from what we call the immortal soul; neither can it be that breath

# Difference between Natural Reason

...breath of life which <term>God</term> breathed into *Adam* (Genesis 2:7) and to which he died immediately upon his transgression. 3. Since the rational mind is gifted <note>original: "endued"</note> with perception and reflection, and since the whole body is capable of feeling <note>original: "sensible"</note> throughout, and because perception and reflection always and immediately accompany and follow sensation, it clearly follows that this power influences the soul and penetrates and pervades the entire body.

4. There are commonly considered to be five senses: namely, seeing, hearing, tasting, smelling, and feeling. Nothing in all of this visible and material world can make an impression, or produce any real sensation or perception in the rational mind, except in and through one or another of them.

5. Therefore, all our real and true knowledge of the things of this visible and material world must be conveyed to the rational mind by the means and intervention of these senses.

6. Consequently, the extent and certainty of all our knowledge regarding the things of this visible and material world must be measured by the number and accuracy of our sensations and perceptions.

7. We find, moreover, that the rational mind is gifted <note>original: "endued"</note> with a power or faculty for retaining the impressions, images, or ideas of all things that are clearly presented to and act upon the outward senses.

8. We find likewise that the rational mind is gifted <note>original: "endued"</note> with a power for variously considering, comparing, combining <note>original: "compounding"</note>, dividing, reflecting upon, and making deductions from these images and ideas.

9. This is what we call <term>ideal knowledge</term>; the variety, extent, and certainty of which is measured by the variety of our sensations and perceptions; by the number of ideas that we retain; by the conformity they hold with the things they represent; and by the accuracy we observe regarding all their various and different relations.

10. From this, it is evident that we can have no true idea of anything except what we first perceived through one or another of the senses.

<meta>This page continues the argument that human knowledge is rooted in sensory experience, specifically addressing how the mind constructs complex ideas and why it possesses no "innate" knowledge from birth.</meta>

# Difference between Natural Reason

...senses. It is true that we may—through analogy, combining <note>original: "composition"</note>, or dividing <note>original: "division"</note>—form ideas for ourselves about things we have never actually perceived with our senses. However, these ideas are only accurate to the extent that the things we *have* perceived at other times (the ideas we are currently using) bear a resemblance or analogy to the new thing we are trying to imagine. Their accuracy also depends on how skilled and successful we are at correctly combining or separating these known ideas. All of this makes this type of knowledge extremely unreliable and uncertain, and therefore hardly worthy of the name "knowledge" at all.

11. From this, it appears that there is no such thing as <term>innate ideas</term> <note>The philosophical theory that the mind is born with certain knowledge or concepts already present, rather than being a blank slate.</note>. For external things must first strike and affect the senses before they can reach the reasoning power that acts upon the soul; they must trigger and stir perception within us before they can imprint their images or ideas upon it.

12. Both our real and ideal knowledge, acquired through the power of reasoning, grow and improve as we increase in years and experience. Thus, the more sensations and perceptions we have become accustomed to, the more images and ideas we store and keep, and the more skillful...

<meta>This page continues the author's resolution of the "innate ideas" debate by distinguishing between knowledge of the physical world (via outward senses) and knowledge of the divine (via an internal divine light).</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>and Divine FAITH.</header>

The truth of the matter, then, is this: since the reasoning power is adapted to the physical and visible world, the mind can have no knowledge of anything relating to it except what it derives in some way, either indirectly <note>original: "mediately"</note> or directly <note>original: "immediately"</note>, from the outward senses. Furthermore, because the soul is the proper recipient <note>original: "subject"</note> of all divine operations, it can neither know nor act in any way consistent with the divine will, except as it is enlightened and empowered by the divine light to do so. 

Just as the physical person <note>original: "outward man"</note> can have no true concept or idea of colors except what he receives through the impressions made upon the eye by means of light, so too the human soul cannot form any concept of "good" without the help of the divine light or life. This light was "inspoken" <term>inspoken: a theological term for a divine truth or life-force implanted directly into the human soul by God</term> through the "seed of the woman" who would bruise the serpent <note>original: "serpent-bruiser." This refers to the messianic prophecy in Genesis 3:15: "And I will put enmity between thee and the woman, and between thy seed and her seed; it shall bruise thy head..."</note>, acting alone in and upon the soul. 

Therefore, those who maintain that all our knowledge is derived from the senses are partially correct. However, they are mistaken when they limit and restrict that knowledge solely to the outward, physical senses. On the other hand, those who say that some part of our knowledge is of a kind that can in no way be derived from impressions made upon the outward senses are certainly correct as well.

35. It is evident then, that there are in man two real and distinct powers or principles that act upon the soul: one suited and adapted to the visible and material world, and the other proper and peculiar to <term>God</term> alone. Because of its excellence, this latter power must have been superior to the former, and was appointed to preside and rule over it. However, it is even more evident that at present most people—or rather all people—despite this natural superiority and pre-eminence, rebel against the dictates and commands of this lawful lord and governor. Instead, they follow and pursue the inclinations <note>original: "bent"</note> of their own inferior appetites and desires, thereby falling into all sorts of irregularities and confusion. It undeniably follows that man is currently corrupted and degenerated; the rights of sovereignty and the duties of subjection between these two powers are miserably perverted, confounded, and weakened <note>original: "enervated"</note>.

When we are governed solely by the first (the natural), we follow all our natural inclinations and desires, doing everything in our power to extinguish the divine light in our souls. In this state, we neither will nor desire anything for ourselves, but are wholly and absolutely surrendered to be moved <note>original: "acted" — meaning to be the passive recipient of God's action</note> and guided by the will of **God** in all things.

### That God alone, through the inspirations of His Holy Spirit, is the guide and director of the souls that desire to be obedient to His teaching; and that in all people there are two internal teachers, each of whom endeavors to guide and direct the soul.

HAVING treated <note>original: "treated of," meaning discussed or examined</note> the two principles that exist in man, and the real and true difference between natural reason, moral reason, and faith—together with the necessary dispositions of the soul to distinguish between each—the next thing that deserves our consideration is the guide whose directions we may and ought to follow. For, as truth and experience teach, there are two internal teachers in every person: namely, 1. The spirit of corrupt nature <note>The "spirit of corrupt nature" refers to the theological concept of the "old man" or the inherent human inclination toward selfish or sinful impulses.</note>. 2. The Divine Spirit. In all our deliberate actions, both of these offer themselves and even strive for mastery, contending which of them,

<summary>This page introduces the theological argument that every person is influenced by two competing internal guides: the Divine Spirit and the spirit of corrupt nature. It asserts that God is the only true guide for those seeking to live obediently.</summary>

page 122
<header>Of the two SPIRITS in Man,</header>
<meta>ink bleed-through from reverse page</meta>

with the exclusion of the other, shall lead us in the ways proper and pleasing to each.

The paths, as well as the destinations to which they lead, are directly opposite to one another. For the spirit of corrupt nature teaches us only those things that are currently pleasing and suitable to our physical <note>original: "carnal"</note> desires, or our sensual and worldly <note>original: "secular"</note> goals. However, these are harmful <note>original: "pernicious"</note> to the soul, or immortal spirit, by leading it into endless errors and mazes <note>original: "labyrinths"</note> that separate it from GOD and true happiness, carrying it instead toward eternal misery. 

On the other hand, the Spirit of GOD reveals <note>original: "discovers"</note> to us the folly and danger of following such a blind and harmful guide as the spirit of nature is. It teaches us that our happiness consists in rejecting such a wandering guide and walking in paths that are quite the opposite: in renouncing present sensual pleasures and gratifications whenever they hinder our knowledge of GOD and spiritual things. These alone should be the object of all our desires and efforts, as only through them can we arrive at eternal happiness and union with GOD.

Besides these two spirits, a person neither has nor can have any other teacher within them, and every person is continually accompanied <note>original: "attended"</note> by both of these.

<header>Each striving for the government <note>meaning the control or rule over the soul</note>.</header>

Whatever, therefore, is not the teaching of the Divine Spirit is the suggestion of the false and evil teacher, who is God’s enemy and our own. This evil teacher took possession of the souls of men upon *Adam's* transgression. Adam's fault was not attending to the teaching of God's Holy Spirit, which at that time was the only internal teacher; instead, he listened <note>original: "hearkening"</note> to the flattering temptations of the woman <note>referring to Eve</note>, who had been seduced by that wicked spirit, the devil. From *Adam*, this corruption of our nature is passed down to all his descendants <note>original: "posterity"</note>, so that in our natural state we have no other teacher. But the Holy Spirit—the new heavenly teacher—is freely given to us by means of the "Bruiser of the Serpent" <note>A traditional title for Christ, based on the prophecy in Genesis 3:15</note>. This is the new divine principle implanted in us to turn our souls back to God, and it is continually urging us to walk in His ways.

Our misery is that, through our own actual mortal sins, the divine light is temporarily almost extinguished, so as not to produce any significant good effect upon the will. However, it is not the same on the other side: through grace, the false teacher is neither totally expelled nor silenced. It remains in the corrupt nature of even the most perfect man; and God knows even the best men are too often inclined to listen to

The ways, as well as the ends to which they lead, are directly contrary to one another. The spirit of corrupt nature only teaches us such things as are presently pleasing and suitable to our physical <note>original: "carnal"</note> desires or worldly <note>original: "secular"</note> designs. However, this is destructive <note>original: "pernicious"</note> to the soul, or immortal spirit, by leading it into endless errors and mazes <note>original: "labyrinths"</note> that separate it from God and true happiness, carrying it instead to eternal misery. 

On the other side, the Spirit of God reveals <note>original: "discovers"</note> to us the folly and danger of following such a blind and destructive guide as the spirit of nature. It teaches us that our happiness consists in rejecting such a wandering guide and walking in paths quite the opposite. This involves renouncing present physical pleasures and gratifications, at least as far as they are a hindrance to our knowing of God and spiritual things. These spiritual matters should be the only object of all our desires and efforts <note>original: "endeavours"</note>, as that is the only way we can arrive at eternal happiness and union with God.

Besides these two spirits, man neither has nor can have any other teacher within him, and every person is continually attended by both.

<vocab>spirit of corrupt nature, immortal spirit, Spirit of GOD, eternal happiness, union with GOD</vocab>

<summary>The author describes the conflicting influences of the Spirit of God and the spirit of nature, arguing that true happiness is found only by rejecting worldly desires and following divine guidance. He concludes that these are the only two internal teachers a human being possesses.</summary>

Aristotle, in the tenth book of his *Ethics*, says, "A virtuous man is the measure and rule of human actions." This belongs to wisdom, which is the gift of the Holy Spirit; and according to the Apostle Paul, the spiritual man judges all things (1 Corinthians 2:15).

Aristotle, though a pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note>, could observe "that for that good which is honest and virtuous, there is first required a certain impulse that cannot be explained by reason; and by this, the mind is able to discern and decide." He further says "that many are driven toward good and virtuous things and achieve them without the exercise of reason. This," he says, "is to be attributed to nature; for such an inclination is naturally imprinted in our souls, by which we are driven toward such things as will make us happy without any exercise of reason. It is so much the case that if one were to ask certain people so inclined, 'Why does it please you to do so?' they would answer, 'I do not know, but it simply pleases me.'"

I. The difference between a <term>PHILOSOPHER</term> <note>original: "PHILOSOPHER" — In this period, a "philosopher" often referred to someone who relied solely on human reason and secular ethics, as opposed to divine revelation.</note> and a <term>CHRISTIAN</term>.
