# TRUE RESIGNATION.

### C H A P. I.

1. We have a clear example in *Lucifer*, and also in *Adam* the first man, of what "self" <note>original: "self"; referring to self-will, the ego, or the human desire for independence from God.</note> does when it takes the light of nature <note>Natural human intellect or the understanding of the physical world.</note> to be its own, and tries to walk with understanding in its own dominion. We see it also in those learned in arts and sciences: when they take the light of this outward world or nature into the possession of their reason, nothing comes of it but pride in themselves. And yet, the whole world intensely desires and seeks after this light as the best treasure; and indeed, it is the best treasure this world affords, if it is used correctly.

2. But while "self"—that is, reason—is ensnared and bound fast in a narrow and strong prison, namely in the anger of God and in earthliness <note>Worldly or carnal attachments.</note>, it is very dangerous for a person

<meta>catchword: to</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the concept of "self" and reason as potential sources of pride, using Lucifer and Adam as examples of how natural intelligence can lead to spiritual downfall when not aligned with God.</summary>
<keywords>Resignation, Lucifer, Adam, self-will, light of nature, reason, pride, anger of God, earthliness</keywords>

--- Page 349 ---

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>Of true RESIGNATION.</header>
<sig>O 5</sig>

to make use of the light of knowledge in self, as if it were in the possession of self.

3. For the wrath of eternal and temporary nature will soon take pleasure in it. Then "self" and a man's own reason will rise up in pride and break away from true, resigned humility toward God. He will no longer eat of the fruit of paradise, but of the property of self—namely, of that dominion of life where good and evil are mixed. This is what *Lucifer* and *Adam* did. Both entered with the desire of "self" back into the original source <note>original: "original"; the primordial state or origin of being.</note> from which the creatures were brought forth, and they entered into the condition of mere creatures. *Lucifer* entered into the center and wrathful nature—into the matrix <note>original: "matrix"; in this mystical context, it refers to the generative womb or underlying source of nature.</note> or womb that brings forth fire. *Adam* entered into the earthly nature, into the matrix of the outward world—namely, into the lust for good and evil.

4. This happened to both of them because they had the light of understanding shining within "self," through which they could behold themselves. Because of this, the spirit of "self" entered into the imagination—specifically, into a desire to reach the center so they might exalt themselves and grow great, powerful, and most skillful. Now when *Lucifer* sought after

...after the <term>mother of fire</term> <note>original: "mother of fire"; in this mystical context, it refers to the generative source or "womb" of the harsh, fiery, and wrathful principle of nature.</note> in his own center. He thought to reign with that fire over the love of <term>God</term> and all the angels. Likewise, when *Adam* also desired to test the essence—to see what existed in the "mother" that produced both evil and good, and from where they originated—he directed his desire into her specifically to become skillful and full of understanding. 

Both *Lucifer* and *Adam* were captivated by their own evil or false desires within this "mother." They broke themselves off from <term>resignation</term> <note>original: "resignation"; the act of surrendering one's self-will and ego to the will of God.</note>, which proceeds from <term>God</term>. Thus, they were captured by the spirit of their own will and by the desire in the "mother," which allowed that desire to gain dominion over their nature. Consequently, *Lucifer* became stuck fast in the wrathful source of fire. That fire became manifest in the spirit of his will, through which he became an enemy to the love and meekness of <term>God</term>.

5. So also was *Adam* caught by the <term>earthly mother</term> <note>original: "earthly mother"; the material or temporal world, which the author views as a mixture of divine love and divine wrath.</note>, which is both evil and good, created out of the love and anger of <term>God</term> and made into one substance. Through this, the earthly property gained dominion over *Adam*. From that point on, heat and cold, envy and anger, and all malice and opposition against <term>God</term> became manifest and ruled within him.

6. 7. We also see now that this brings danger to the enlightened children of <term>God</term>. When the sun of the great presence of <term>God</term>’s holiness shines—through which life passes into triumph and reason beholds itself as if in a mirror—the will often continues in "self" (that is, in its own investigation). It tries to determine for itself what the center is from which the light shines and attempts to force its way into it. Then, abominable pride and self-love arise, so that the creature's reason, which is merely a mirror or reflection of the eternal wisdom, imagines itself to be greater than it is. Consequently, whatever its reason does, it thinks <term>God</term>’s will is doing through it, and that it is a prophet. Yet reason remains trapped in the "self" and follows its own desire. In this state, the center of nature suddenly rises up and enters into that selfish desire, which is a falsehood against <term>God</term>; thus, the will enters into self-conceit.

9. Yet even then, the outward light of reason—or the light of his outward nature—continues to shine in the person. The "self" throws itself into that natural light and then falsely supposes it to be the primary light of God. But it is not so. Into this vanity <note>original: "self-conceitedness"</note> regarding the light of his own outward reason, the devil throws himself again (even though he was forced to depart during the first light, which was divine). He returns with a sevenfold desire, of which Christ spoke in *Matthew 12:43–45*, saying: *“When the unclean spirit departs from a man, he wanders through dry places, seeking rest and finding none. Then he says, 'I will return to my house from which I came.' And when he arrives, he finds it empty, swept, and put in order. 10. This house, which is swept and decorated in this way, represents the light of reason within the self. By this, the external light of reason is also ignited; for where the light of God is, everything will be light. The devil cannot remain there; he must depart. He then searches through the source of life's origin <note>original: "mother of the original of life"</note>—namely, the "center"—but finds it has become a dry and weak place. The anger of God—which is the <term>center of nature</term> <note>Böhme uses "center of nature" to describe the core of a person's being where raw, unrefined desires and the "fire" of existence reside.</note>—is, in its own quality, completely weak, barren, lean, and dry. Satan searches through those places to find an open gate to enter with his desire, trying to sift the soul so that it might begin to exalt itself.

11. Then the devil finds an open gate to enter, and a decorated house to live in.

12. Then this man continues in the light of outward reason—which is called the <term>stars</term>—and perceives great and wonderful things, and finds a constant guide in them. But then the devil immediately observes where any gate of the mind stands open for him, where he may ignite the <term>center of life</term>, so that the spirit of the will may rise up in pride, in self-conceit, or in covetousness. From this, self-arrogance arises; the will of reason desires to be honored, for it imagines it has attained the height of all happiness once it has acquired the light of reason. The creature <note>original: "creature"; Böhme uses this to refer to the human being as a created thing, often emphasizing the person's dependence on God.</note> now supposes that the honor is due to him because he has gained the understanding of reason.

### *The way that a true Christian must follow.*

16. Reason will object and say: "Is it not right for a person to attain the light of God, and also the light of outward nature and reason, so that they may be able to order their life wisely, as the scripture says?"

17. Yes, it is very right; nothing can be more profitable to a person, nor can any better thing happen to them. Indeed, it is a treasure above all earthly treasures for a person to have the light of God and of time <note>original: "time"; in Böhme's writing, "time" refers to the temporal, natural world or the span of physical life.</note> (or reason), for it is the eye of both time and eternity.

18. But note how you ought to use it: the light of God first manifests itself in the soul; it shines forth like light from a candle and immediately kindles the outward light of reason. Yet, it does not yield itself wholly up to reason to be under the dominion of the outward man <note>The "outward man" refers to the physical body and the ego-driven personality that interacts with the material world.</note>. No, the outward man beholds himself in this light, through its shining radiance, just as he sees his likeness in a mirror; he may immediately learn to know himself, which is good and profitable for him.

19. And when he does so, reason—which is the creaturely self <note>The "creaturely self" is the individual's identity as a created being, distinct from the Creator.</note>—cannot do better than to behold itself in the "self" of the creature, and not to enter with the will of its desire into the...

<meta>catchword: center,</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that while the light of God is the highest treasure, it is meant to illuminate and guide human reason rather than be controlled by it. He uses the metaphor of a candle and a mirror to show how divine light allows the "outward man" to achieve true self-knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>light of God, light of reason, self-knowledge, outward man, creaturely self, Jacob Böhme, resignation, divinity</keywords>

->center, in seeking itself; if it does this, it breaks itself off from the substance of GOD (which arises together with the light of GOD, and which the soul ought to feed upon and refresh itself with) and instead feeds on outward substance and light, and thereby draws venom into itself again.<-

20. The will of the creature <note>original: "creature"; here meaning the created human being as distinct from the Creator.</note> ought to sink wholly into itself, with all its reason and desire, accounting itself an unworthy child that is not at all worthy of such high grace. Nor should it claim any knowledge or understanding for itself, or desire or beg of GOD to have any understanding within its own creaturely self. Instead, it should sincerely and simply immerse itself in the grace and love of GOD in CHRIST JESUS, and desire to be, as it were, dead to itself and its own reason within the divine life. It should wholly resign itself to the life of GOD in love, so that He may do however and whatever He wills with it, as if it were His own instrument.

21. Its own reason ought not to engage in any speculation regarding divine things, or the ground and foundation of human matters; nor should it will or desire anything but the grace of GOD in CHRIST. And as a child continually longs for the breasts of the mother,

<meta>This page continues the metaphor of a child longing for its mother’s milk, comparing it to the soul’s persistent hunger for God's love. It emphasizes the need for total self-abandonment to allow the Holy Spirit to govern the individual.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>Of true RESIGNATION.</header>

...mother, so must its hunger <note>original: "hunger"; here referring to the soul’s deep spiritual longing or appetite.</note> continually enter into the love of <term>God</term>. It must not allow itself to be cut off from this hunger by any means, even when external reason triumphs in the light, saying, "I have the true child." <note>This likely refers to the "new birth" or the presence of Christ within the soul.</note> Instead, the will of the desire must bow itself down to the earth and bring itself into the deepest humility and simple ignorance, saying: "You are foolish and have nothing but the grace of <term>God</term>. You must wrap yourself up in that grace with great humility and become nothing at all in yourself, neither knowing nor loving yourself." All that you have, or all that is within you, must regard itself as nothing but a mere instrument of <term>God</term>. Then you must direct your desire only toward <term>God's</term> mercy, moving away from all your own knowing and willing and regarding them as nothing at all; nor must you ever entertain any will to return to them again.

22. As soon as this is done, the natural will becomes weak, and then the Devil is no longer able to sift <note>original: "sift"; an allusion to Luke 22:31, meaning to shake or test one's faith through temptation.</note> it with his evil (or false) desire. For the places where he rests become very weak (or fruitless, or barren) and dry. Then the Holy Spirit, proceeding from <term>God</term>, takes possession of the forms of life <note>original: "forms of life"; a term used to describe the fundamental qualities or structures that constitute a person's existence.</note> and causes His dominion to prevail.

<summary>This page explains that the soul must remain in a state of deep humility and "simple ignorance," viewing itself only as an instrument of God. By abandoning the personal will and self-importance, the individual weakens the influence of the Devil and allows the Holy Spirit to take control of their life.</summary>

26. But the will of the soul must continually sink into nothingness—that is, into the deepest humility in the sight of <term>GOD</term>. Therefore, the soul must remain close to resigned humility, just as a well stays close to its spring; it must draw and drink from **God's** fountain and not depart from the ways of **GOD** at all.

27. This influence immediately seizes the soul and makes it spiritually dry. The soul then wanders in error until it yields itself up again in resignation and acknowledges once more that it is a defiled child. By resisting its own reason and thus regaining the love of <term>GOD</term>—which is harder to do now than it was at first—it returns to the right path. For the powerful Devil brings strong doubts; he will not easily abandon his captured prey.

28. This can be seen clearly in the saints of <term>GOD</term> from the beginning of the world: many who were led by the Spirit of <term>GOD</term> still often drifted from resignation into "self"—namely, into their own reason and will. In that state, Satan cast them into sin and into the anger of <term>GOD</term>, as is evident in the lives of **David** and **Solomon**, and also the Patriarchs, Prophets, and Apostles. They often committed great errors when they moved away from resignation and into "self"—that is, into their own reason and lust <note>original: "lust"; in this context, it refers to any selfish, worldly, or disordered desire, not exclusively sexual desire.</note>.

29. Therefore, it is necessary for the children of <term>GOD</term> to know how they should conduct themselves.

<summary>Böhme warns that when the soul relies on human reason and ego rather than divine surrender, it becomes spiritually dry and prone to error. 34. When the will dies to itself <note>original: "dies from itself"; the process of surrendering one's ego or self-centered desires.</note>, it becomes free from sin. This is because it no longer desires anything except what **God** desires for His creatures; it only wishes to do what **God** created it for and what **God** intends to accomplish through it. Although the person is the one performing the action, they are merely the instrument of that action, through which **God** carries out His own will.

35. This is what true faith in a person looks like: dying to oneself—that is, to one's own selfish desires. In all of one's plans and undertakings, a person should align their desires with the will of **God**. They should not claim credit <note>original: "arrogate to himself"; to take or claim something without justification.</note> for doing anything by their own power. Instead, in everything they do, they should consider themselves to be merely a servant or minister <note>original: "minister"; in this context, a helper or agent acting on behalf of another.</note> of **God**, believing that everything they do or undertake is for **God's** sake. With such an intention, the Spirit of **God** leads them into true integrity and faithfulness toward their neighbor. Such a person thinks to themselves: "I do my work not for my own benefit, but for **God**, who has called and appointed me to do it, like a servant in His vineyard." They listen constantly for the voice of their Master, who commands them from within what they should do; the **Lord** speaks inside them and directs them to act.

Instead, it is in the dominion of nature, through which **God** governs both evil and good; and nothing is divine unless it lives and works within the will of **God**.

4. Every creature must realize that it should remain in the condition in which it was created. Otherwise, it follows a contrary will, enters into enmity with the will of <term>GOD</term>, and brings itself into pain. For a creature that is created out of darkness feels no pain in the darkness; just as a venomous worm feels no pain in its own venom, because that venom is its life. If it were to lose its venom and have something good put into it instead—and if that good were to be made manifest in its <term>essence</term>—this would cause it pain and death. In the same way, evil is pain and death to that which is good.

6. This pain is an eternal despairing of **God's** grace and a continual hostility. Because **God** cannot tolerate him within Himself, He has "spewed him out." This nature now keeps him prisoner in his own place as a rebel or fallen spirit, and it sustains itself within him through its qualities of anger and wrath. And since he refused to promote the delight of divine joy, he must now do the opposite and be an enemy of all goodness. For all things come from **God** and exist within Him—darkness and light, love and anger, fire and light—but He calls Himself "**God**" only in relation to the light of His love.

7. There is an eternal opposition between darkness and light; neither of them contains the other. Yet, there is only one single essence, being, or substance in which they both exist. There is a difference in their quality and will, yet that essence or substance is not divided. Instead, a <term>principle</term> <note>Boehme uses "principle" to describe a fundamental state of being or a dimension of existence (e.g., the dark world, the light world, and the external world).</note> creates the division, so that one is as nothing within the other—and yet it is there, though not revealed within the nature of the thing in which it resides.

8. For the Devil remained in his own dominion or principality, but not in the one where **GOD** originally created him. Instead, he stayed in the aching, painful birth of eternity—in the center of nature and the quality of wrath, the quality which produces darkness, anguish, and pain. Indeed, he is a prince in this world's domain, yet he exists within the <term>first principle</term> <note>The "first principle" in Boehme’s theology is the dark, fiery root of nature, which is hidden when the light of God (the second principle) shines through it.</note> in the kingdom of darkness, in the pit or abyss.

9. But he is not a lord or prince in the kingdom of the sun, stars, and elements. He is no lord or prince there, except in the wrathful part—specifically, in the root of evil within everything—and yet he does not have the power to do whatever he pleases with it.

11. For the origin of the soul and of all angels is from the same eternal nature. But the Devil has no power over time or the temporary conditions of this world, except within the curse. Wherever that curse ignites itself in eternal and natural wrath, he is busy—such as in wars, fighting, and strife, as well as in great <term>tempests</term> <note>Original: "tempests"; refers to violent storms.</note>. In fire, he proceeds only as far as the mischief or harm goes; he is present in great showers and storms of thunder, lightning, and hail, but he cannot direct them. He is not the lord and master of them, but merely a servant.

13. Now GOD created everything for, and in that state where it should be: the angels for and in heaven, and man for and in paradise. If, therefore, the desire of the creature goes out from its own mother <note>original: "mother"; here referring to the origin or source of the creature’s existence</note>, then it enters into a contrary will and into enmity. It is tormented with the conflict therein, and so a false will arises within a good one. Then the good will returns into its "nothing" <note>original: "nothing"; in Boehme’s philosophy, this refers to the *Ungrund*, the absolute, undifferentiated source of all being beyond nature</note> again—namely, into the end of nature and the creature—and so leaves the creature in its own evil or wickedness, as appears by the examples of *Lucifer* and also *Adam*. rest. For <term>self-desire</term> <note>original: "self-desire"; the selfish, ego-driven will that seeks its own ends rather than God's</note> always brings unrest. If the desire of anger in the earthly flesh then aligns or joins with the devil’s imagination and attacks the will of the soul, the resigned desire cries out to <term>God</term> and says, "<term>Abba</term> <note>original: "Abba"; an Aramaic word for "Father," used here to show a child-like, intimate trust in God</note>, loving Father, deliver me from this evil!" Then, even if the earthly will should grow too strong in the <term>wrath of God</term> <note>original: "wrath of God"; in Boehme’s theology, this refers to the harsh, fire-like quality of nature when it is not tempered by divine love</note> because of the devil's corruption, the desire of anger would only act upon itself. As the Apostle says: *“Now if I do what I do not want, it is no longer I who do it, but sin that dwells in me”* <note>original: Romans 7:20</note>; and also, *“I myself serve the law of God with my mind, but with my flesh I serve the law of sin”* <note>original: Romans 7:25</note>. 

The Apostle does not mean that the soul’s will should consent in the slightest to the will of the flesh. Instead, he means that sin is often so powerful in the flesh that it awakens God's anger within the self, and the person is forced into lustful desires by the evil temptations of wicked people or by witnessing worldly pomp and glory. In such cases, the resigned will is overwhelmed and ruled by force.

Mere physical actions do not make a person a child of **GOD**. Instead, it is the internal working of **CHRIST** within the spirit that makes one—and truly is—the child of **GOD**. This inner activity is so powerful that it shines through outward actions like a new light, revealing the person as a child of **GOD** even in their physical life.

39. For if the "eye" of the soul is full of light, then the entire body and all its members are enlightened. If anyone boasts of being a child of **GOD** while allowing their body to burn in sin, they are not yet capable of being His child; rather, they are held captive in the chains of the devil in great darkness. If a person does not find within themselves a sincere, burning desire to do good out of love, then their claim is merely a product of human logic <note>original: "reason"; here referring to the intellect of the "self" which operates apart from divine grace</note> coming from the self. This self-centered nature cannot see **GOD** unless it is born again and demonstrates through its own power and virtue that it is His child. For there is no fire that does not produce light; if the divine fire is in the mind, it will shine forth, and the mind will do what **GOD** wants done.

40. But perhaps you will say, "I truly have the will to do so, and I would gladly do it, but I am so hindered that I cannot."

...where is that child who believes that **JESUS** is born? If that child truly existed <note>original: "were in being"</note> and did believe that **JESUS** is born, it would also draw near to the sweet child **JESUS**, and receive him, and nurture him.

52. Alas! Faith nowadays is merely historical, a simple knowledge of the story: that the **Jews** killed him, that he left this world, and that he is not king on earth over the <term>animal man</term> <note>Boehme's term for the natural, unrefined human nature driven by instinct and ego rather than spirit.</note>. Instead, people think they may do as they please <note>original: "as they list"</note> and need not die to sin and their evil desires. The wicked "self" rejoices in all of this, so that it may fatten the **Devil** by living in worldly luxury.

53. This shows plainly that true faith has never been weaker or feebler since **CHRIST's** time than it is now. Nevertheless, the world cries aloud and says, "We have found the true faith!" and they argue about the "child" <note>The image of the newly born spirit within the believer.</note> so fiercely that there has never been worse conflict since humanity began.

54. If you have that newborn child who was lost and is found again, then let it be seen in your power and virtue. Let us openly see the sweet child **JESUS** brought forth by you, and see that you are his nurse. If not, then the children in **CHRIST** will say...

<summary>Boehme distinguishes between "historical faith"—a mere intellectual belief in scripture—and a true, living faith where Christ is spiritually reborn within the individual. He argues that true faith must be manifested through a person's virtues and a death to sinful desires, rather than through religious arguments.</summary>

# THE
# PARABLE
# OF A
# PILGRIM.

1. There was a man who had a great desire to go to *Jerusalem*. Because he did not know the right way, he turned for advice to someone he hoped was skilled <note>original: "unskilful," used here in the double negative "not unskilful" to mean expert or knowledgeable</note> in the matter. He asked him if there was any passable way to get there. The other answered that the way was both long and full of very great difficulties. Indeed, he said that there were many paths that seemed to lead there and promised to do so, but the uncertainty and danger of them were too great. Nevertheless, he knew of one way that—if followed diligently according to the directions and landmarks he would provide—could be traveled safely and led straight there. "I cannot," he said, "promise you safety <note>original: "Security"</note> from many scares, beatings, and other forms of mistreatment, nor from temptations of all kinds."

--- Page 401 ---
<meta>This is the opening page of the book, establishing the central allegory of a spiritual journey. The text introduces the protagonist and his guide, a common trope in 17th-century devotional literature.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal headpiece consisting of two rows of stylized floral or foliate scrolls.</image-desc>

# THE
# PARABLE
# OF A
# PILGRIM.

1. There was a man who had a great desire to go to *Jerusalem* <note>In this context, Jerusalem represents the "Heavenly City" or a state of spiritual perfection, a common goal in Christian allegories.</note>. Because he did not know the right way, he turned for advice to someone he hoped was skilled in finding it, asking him if there was any passable way to get there. The other person answered that the way was both long and full of very great difficulties; indeed, there were many paths that seemed and promised to lead there, but the uncertainty and danger of them were too great. Nevertheless, there was one way he knew which—if followed diligently according to the directions and landmarks <note>original: "marks"</note> he would provide—could be traveled safely and led straight there. "I cannot, however," he said, "promise you <term>security</term> <note>meaning: safety or protection</note> from many scares, beatings, and other harsh treatment, nor from temptations of every kind."

<summary>A man seeking the spiritual path to Jerusalem asks a guide for directions. The guide warns him that while a direct path exists, the journey is long, dangerous, and filled with trials.</summary>
<keywords>parable, pilgrim, Jerusalem, spiritual journey, guidance, temptation, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 402 ---
<meta>This page continues the Guide's instructions to the Pilgrim, emphasizing the mental state required for the journey: a mantra of total humility and singular focus on the heavenly goal.</meta>

->### The Parable of a PILGRIM.<-

...troubles of all kinds. But if you can have enough courage and patience to endure them, without complaining, resisting, or troubling yourself, and keep going, having this only in your mind and sometimes on your tongue: **"I HAVE NOTHING; I AM NOTHING; I DESIRE NOTHING BUT TO BE AT JERUSALEM."** <note>original: "I HAVE NOUGHT: I AM NOUGHT: I DESIRE NOUGHT, BUT TO BE AT JERUSALEM." This mantra is a traditional exercise in humility, stripping away worldly identity and possessions to focus entirely on the spiritual goal.</note> I promise you on my life <note>original: "My life for thine," an idiomatic expression meaning "I guarantee it with my own life."</note> that you will escape safely with your life and, in a reasonable <note>original: "competent"</note> amount of time, arrive at that city.

§ 2. The Pilgrim, overjoyed with this news, answered, "As long as I may keep my life safe and finally come to the place that I desire above all things, I do not care what hardships <note>original: "miseries"</note> I suffer on my way. Therefore, let me know only what course I am to take, and, God willing, I will not fail to observe your directions carefully." The Guide replied, "Since you have such a good will—even though I myself was never so fortunate <note>original: "happy"</note> as to be in *Jerusalem*—be confident that through the instructions I shall give you, if you will follow them, you will come safely to your journey's end."

§ 3. Now, the advice that I am to give you in brief is this. Before you set the first step onto the highway that leads to *Jerusalem*, you must be firmly grounded in the love of God and faith in Jesus Christ; moreover, whatever sins you find in your conscience... <meta>The sentence continues on the next page with the word "conscience."</meta>

<summary>The Guide provides the Pilgrim with a mantra of humility to survive the journey's hardships and explains that the first steps of the path require firm faith and a clear conscience.</summary>
<keywords>humility, spiritual discipline, Jerusalem, Guide, Pilgrim, faith, repentance, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 403 ---
<meta>This page continues the Guide's instructions to the Pilgrim. The previous page ended with: "...whatever sins you find in your conscience..."</meta>

->### The Parable of a PILGRIM.<-

...conscience, you must seek to purge them away through hearty, sincere repentance. Once this is done, begin your journey in God’s name; but be sure to go provided with two necessary tools or companions: namely, Humility and Charity. Both of these will unite in the statement mentioned before, which must always be ready in your mind: **I AM NOTHING; I HAVE NOTHING; I DESIRE ONLY ONE THING, AND THAT IS OUR LORD JESUS CHRIST, AND TO BE WITH HIM IN PEACE AT JERUSALEM.** 

You must therefore keep the meaning and power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of these words continually in your thoughts, either specifically or in spirit. Humility says, *I am nothing; I have nothing.* Love says, *I desire nothing but* JESUS CHRIST. You must never part from these two companions, nor will they willingly be separated from one another, for they harmonize beautifully together. The more deeply you ground yourself in humility, the higher you raise yourself in charity; for the more you see and feel yourself to be nothing but a worm <note>A common self-deprecating metaphor in spiritual texts of this period, often referencing Psalm 22:6 to express extreme humility before God.</note>, the more fervently you will desire Christ—so that through Him, who is ALL, you may become something that is acceptable to God.

<summary>The guide instructs the pilgrim to use repentance, humility, and charity as essential tools for the journey, centered on a mantra of self-negation and a singular focus on Christ.</summary>

<keywords>Humility, Charity, Repentance, Jerusalem, Jesus Christ, Pilgrim, Spiritual Journey, Self-denial</keywords>

--- Page 404 ---
<meta>This page continues the Guide's instructions on the two tools for the journey: Humility and Charity. It specifically elaborates on how to achieve a deeper, more spiritual form of humility by focusing on God rather than one's own sins.</meta>

->### The Parable of a PILGRIM.<-

4. Now, this same humility is to be practiced, not so much by focusing on yourself—your sinfulness and your misery (although doing this at the very beginning is good and profitable)—but rather through a quiet, loving gaze at the infinite, endless being and goodness of GOD, through his Son JESUS CHRIST. 

This observation of God's love must come either through grace—by a felt knowledge of CHRIST formed within you through the new birth—or at least through a full and firm faith in him. Such a gaze, when you attain it, will produce in your mind a far more pure, spiritual, solid, and perfect humility than the previous method of looking at yourself. That other way produces a humility that is more crude, turbulent, and restless <note>original: "gross, boisterous and unquiet." In this context, "boisterous" suggests a rough or disordered state of mind, while "unquiet" refers to a lack of spiritual peace.</note>.

### That the Will, being dead to itself, lives entirely for the Will of God.

1. The French speak with a unique appropriateness <note>original: "singular propriety"</note> concerning the death of men, for they call it an "overpassing," and the dead, the "overpassed" <note>original: *trépas* and *trépassé*, suggesting a crossing over or transition</note>. This suggests that death is nothing more than a transition from one life to another; to die is simply to cross beyond the boundaries of this life to reach a state of immortality. It is true that our will can no more die than our soul can; yet, the will sometimes goes beyond the limits of its ordinary life to live entirely within the divine will. At that point, the will neither can nor wants to desire anything at all. Instead, it resigns and gives itself up totally and without reserve to the good pleasure of the **Divine Spirit**, so that it may be united to **Jesus Christ**, and so that its life may be hidden with him in **God**. There, the will lives no longer by its own desires, but by the will of **Jesus Christ**, whose image it then bears.

2. What happens to the brightness of the stars when the sun appears on our horizon?

<summary>This page explores the mystical concept of the "death of the will," comparing it to the French term for death as a transition or "overpassing." It describes how a soul surrenders its own desires to live entirely through the will of Christ.</summary>

[horizon]? Certainly, their light is in no way diminished, but it unites with and is happily received into the sun's superior splendor. In the same way, when a person's will is entirely surrendered and resigned to <term>GOD</term>'s good pleasure, it does not perish. Instead, it is happily joined in that union for which the soul was originally created. It no longer acts separately, but within the will of <term>GOD</term>; it does not appear as its own entity, as it is absorbed into the divine will.

3. A soul perfectly resigned to <term>GOD</term> should not have any will of its own, but simply follow the will of <term>GOD</term>. Just as a passenger on a ship does not progress through a voyage by their own motion, but allows themselves to be carried by the movement of the vessel they are in; so the soul embarked on the "divine pleasure" <note>original: "divine pleasure," referring to God's sovereign will or what is pleasing to Him</note> ought to have no other will than to let itself be guided by the divine will. At that stage, the will no longer says, as it did before, "Your will be done, not mine," for there is no longer a separate will left to be renounced. Instead, it speaks to this effect: "<term>LORD</term>, I place my whole will and the desire of my soul into your hands, as if I no longer had a will of my own to manage; it is now entirely at the disposal of your divine will." ### Of Humanity's <note>original: "Man's"</note> Happiness in being commanded by **GOD** to love Him above all things.

1. A human being <note>original: "MAN"</note> in a state of spiritual rebirth <note>original: "regenerate state"</note> is the most perfect creature in the universe. Therefore, the love of **GOD** is the ultimate goal <note>original: "the end," meaning the final purpose or destination</note> of all perfection.

This commandment is like a sun, giving light <note>original: "lustre"</note> and dignity to all the holy laws, divine decrees <note>original: "ordinances"</note>, and instructions contained in the Scriptures. Everything is designed to promote this heavenly love, and everything leads toward it. ### Wherein the Force and Excellence of Holy Love Consists.

1. How great an extent, then, ought the force of this sacred love of God above all things to have? It is to surpass all our other affections <note>original: "affections" — used here to mean emotional attachments, passions, or desires</note> and to vanquish all our difficulties. It is to prefer the honor and happiness of God's favor above all things—yes, I say, before all things absolutely, without any exception or reservation at all. I say this with the greatest care, because there are men who would courageously forsake their goods, honors, and even life itself for their religious principles, yet who will not follow their master, Jesus Christ, nor leave behind things of far less consequence for his sake.

2. It is therefore true that it is not enough for us to love God more than our temporal <note>original: "temporal" — relating to worldly time or physical life, as opposed to spiritual or eternal life</note> life, unless we also love him generally, absolutely, and without reserve;

<meta>This page continues the argument that divine love must be absolute, addressing whether martyrdom is the sole measure of such love.</meta>

# Wherein the Force and Excellence of Holy Love Consists.

...more than all we do, or can love besides. But you may perhaps say: Did our Savior not define the highest point of our love toward him by telling his disciples that *no one has greater love than this, to lay down one's life for one's friends?* <note>See John 15:13.</note> It is true indeed that among the specific acts and testimonies of divine love, there is none so great as to undergo death for the glory of God. Yet it is also true that this is only one single act and one single testimony, even though it is indeed the highest single act of true charity <term>original: "charity" — derived from the Latin *caritas*, meaning selfless, divine love rather than just giving alms</term>. 

But charity requires many things of us and is intended to regulate all our actions. The more fervent our love for God is, the more easily it will guide the common and ordinary actions of our lives toward the love of our neighbor. This is because loving our neighbor is generally more necessary for the preservation of divine love. 

To be willing to die for our profession of faith while excluding the love of our neighbor is not divine charity, but vanity. Charity is not impractical <note>original: "fantastical" — here meaning based on whim or imagination rather than reality</note>; yet she would be so to the highest degree if, while being resolved to please God in matters of the greatest difficulty, she permitted a soul to displease him in matters of less mo- <meta>catchword: ment.</meta>

<summary>The author explains that while dying for one's faith is the highest single act of love, true divine charity must also regulate everyday life and include love for one's neighbor.</summary>

### That the Love of God produces the Love of our Neighbor.

Just as God originally created humanity in his own image and likeness, he ordained that we should love that image as a reflection of the love that is due to the Creator's own divinity. This is why our Lord, when asked about our duty to God, answered: *“You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart: This is the first and greatest commandment; and the second is like it: You shall love your neighbor as yourself.”* Why do we love God? Because he is the supreme <note>original: "most sovereign"</note> and infinite goodness. And why do we love others through charity? It is surely because all people are capable of being restored, through spiritual renewal <note>original: "regeneration"</note>, to the image and likeness of God. And since all people are endowed <note>original: "endued"</note> with the power to turn their...

<keywords>charity, image of God, commandments, neighborly love, divinity, regeneration, spiritual renewal</keywords>

bring the desire of their mind to love God, and thereby are capable to be advanced to the dignity of being coheirs *with* Christ, we love our neighbor as the most holy and living <note>original: "lively"</note> image of the divine. For it is in that capacity <note>original: "quality"</note>, and in that alone, that we belong to God in such a close alliance and such a lovely <note>original: "amiable"</note> dependence that he does not hesitate to be called Father; and when we are so united to him in Christ, he calls us his children. It is in this capacity that we are to be united to his divine essence, by the enjoyment <note>original: "fruition"</note> of his supreme <note>original: "sovereign"</note> generosity <note>original: "bounty"</note> and happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note>. ### Christians ought to go over the course of their race in this world with care and exactness.

Those who desire to lead the life of a Christian with great precision <note>original: "exactness"</note>, the very first thing they are obliged to take care of is that leading power in the soul by which we discern and understand things. Once we have attained a true understanding of the difference between good and evil—always distinguishing things that are foreign <note>original: "introduced into"</note> and contrary to our original nature—our conduct <term>conversation</term> in the world may be always upright and blameless <note>original: "inoffensive"</note>. By making use of this discerning power, we may keep ourselves free from any engagement or "covenant" with the suggestions of sin. Being rewarded with the heavenly gift for doing so, we may become worthy of the **Lord**. The body bears a clear reflection <note>original: "fair resemblance"</note> of the soul, and the things which are seen reflect the things which are invisible. For just as the body has the eye for its guide, and this same guide, by its sight, conducts...

## Christians should run their race in this world with care and precision.

Those who desire to live a Christian life with great precision must first take care of the soul's guiding power—our ability to discern and understand. By properly distinguishing between good and evil, and by always recognizing things that are alien <note>original: "introduced into"</note> and contrary to our original nature, our conduct <term>conversation</term> <note>In this period, "conversation" referred to one's entire manner of living or social conduct, not just speech.</note> in the world will always be upright and without fault. By using this power of discernment, we can keep ourselves from forming any bond or agreement with the temptations of sin. Having done so, we may be rewarded with the heavenly gift and become worthy of the Lord. The body is a clear reflection of the soul, just as visible things represent those that are invisible. For just as the body has the eye as its guide, and this guide, through its sight, directs

But because they have nothing more than a physical, outward righteousness <note>original: "righteousness of the flesh"</note>, they do not belong to the Holy Spirit.

However fine and subtle <note>original: "subtil," meaning ethereal or non-material</note> those bodies may be, they still have substance, form, and image according to their nature. 

The soul also has its own spiritual body, through which it sees, hears, speaks, acts, and performs all the functions <note>original: "offices"</note> of life. When the soul is renewed and sanctified by the Holy Spirit, it is united to the Lord and becomes a sharer of the glory which is incorruptible.

<page-num>42</page-num>

<header>It is GOD who works through</header>

->**It is God who works wonders through his saints.**<-

Who was it that shut the gates of heaven? Elijah <note>original: "Elias," the Greek form of the Hebrew name for the prophet Elijah</note>. Or was it the Spirit of God in him that commanded the rain itself? My opinion is that the one in whom the power of heaven resides was himself seated within the mind of Elijah, and that it was the Word of God through Elijah's tongue that forbade the rain so that it should not fall upon the earth; and again he spoke, and the windows of heaven were opened, and the rain came down.

In the same way, Moses also threw down his staff <note>original: "rod"</note>, and it became a serpent. And again, he spoke, and it became a staff as before. He took ashes out of the furnace and scattered them, and they became boils <note>Referring to the sixth plague of Egypt described in Exodus 9</note>. He struck the ground, and there came up lice and frogs. Was human nature able to do these things on its own? He spoke to the sea, and it was divided.

<summary>This page argues that the miracles performed by biblical figures like Elijah and Moses were not the result of human power, but of God dwelling within and acting through them. It uses these examples to illustrate how the divine Word can be united with a human soul to exercise heavenly power.</summary>

Joshua, the son of Nun, when he came to Jericho, besieged it for seven days without being able to accomplish anything; but when God gave the command, the walls collapsed on their own. And when Joshua entered the Promised Land, the LORD said to him, "Go to the battle." Joshua answered, "As the LORD lives, I will not go without You." And who was it that commanded the sun to stand still two hours longer during the heat of the battle? Was it Joshua in his human nature, or a Power present with him? In truth, it was the Word of God, who is GOD.

How was David able to fight a giant in battle without weapons, as he did? When David threw the stone against the foreigner <note>original: "the alien," referring to the Philistine Goliath</note> with his hand, the hand of GOD guided the stone, and the divine Power itself killed Goliath and achieved <note>original: "wrought"</note> the victory. <page-num>44</page-num>

# It is GOD who works through His Saints

David could not have done this by his own strength, for he was physically weak. 

When you hear of these things being done, do not let your mind wander far from the point. Since these events were types and shadows <note>original: "types and shadows"; these are Old Testament events seen as symbols or prefigurations of spiritual truths</note> of true spiritual realities, apply these things to yourself. For whenever you stretch out the "hands" of your mind and your thoughts toward heaven, and are willing to hold fast to the Lord, Satan will then be inferior to your thoughts. By the power of God, your thoughts shall prevail. 

And just as the walls of *Jericho* fell down through the power of God, so now shall the walls of sin that block the mind fall; the cities of Satan and all your enemies shall be destroyed through the power of God. In this way, the power of God was—and is now—constantly present with the righteous, working wonders through the divine Word dwelling within them.

In the same way, the divine Word exerted its influence in the Prophets. The Word provided <note>original: "administered"</note> the Spirit to their souls so that they might prophesy and speak when there were great things to be proclaimed to the world. They did not speak at all times, but only at the pleasure of the Word, and as the Spirit gave them 

<meta>catchword: them</meta>

<summary>This page explains that Old Testament miracles, like the fall of Jericho, are spiritual symbols for how God's power can destroy sin within a believer's mind. It also describes how the divine Word and Spirit empowered the Prophets to speak.</summary>

<keywords>typology, Jericho, spiritual warfare, David, Prophets, Holy Spirit, indwelling Word</keywords>

--- Page 455 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the divine Word empowered the Prophets and transitions to the greater availability of the Holy Spirit under the New Covenant.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>

->[God] works wonders through His Saints.<-

...them power; nevertheless, the Word was present with them at all times.

Therefore, if the Holy Spirit was given in such a high degree to the "shadow" <note>original: "shadow"; referring to the Old Covenant or the Law as a precursor to Christ</note>, how much more will it be given in the New Covenant, following the coming of Christ and his death on the cross, where there is the greatest outpouring <note>original: "effusion"</note> of the Spirit? "*I will pour out my Spirit*," says God, "*upon all flesh*." And our blessed Lord confirmed this, saying, "*I will be with you to the end of the world*," and "*everyone who seeks, finds*." For, he says, "*If you, then, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your heavenly Father give the Holy Spirit to those who ask Him!*" Let everyone, then, ask in the full assurance of faith.

However, this Spirit is received and experienced in various degrees <note>original: "by measure"</note>, according to how we ask in line with the will of God, and as we live in patience and love toward Him. As the apostle writes to the *Hebrews*, the senses of the soul are "trained <note>original: "exercised"</note> to distinguish both good and evil." This experience also comes through the various gifts of grace and by the help of the energy and power of the Spirit. For anyone who knows the treachery of sin—how it defiles the soul through wicked passions <note>original: "vile affections"</note>—and yet ignores the help offered by the Spirit of truth within him,

<meta>catchword: to</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the Holy Spirit is more abundantly available under the New Covenant than in the Old Testament and explains that believers receive this power in varying degrees based on their faith and spiritual discipline.</summary>

<keywords>Holy Spirit, New Covenant, effusion, grace, prayer, Hebrews, spiritual discernment, sin, Spirit of truth</keywords>

--- Page 456 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that true spiritual transformation requires the active participation of the individual and cannot be forced by fear or granted as a simple legal pardon without internal change.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>A LETTER to the PUBLISHER.</header>

...there is [little] hope at the end, when the whole life has been habitually wrong before. Evil must be done away with, and good must take shape in the soul so that it becomes the soul's own choice and its prevailing principle. This must happen during a "season of freedom"—when the mind is not influenced by fear or dread—for a time of fear is not a time of true liberty. Therefore, when the pressure of fear is removed, the soul (like a spring) returns to its former state. Nothing stays or is incorporated into the soul (if one may put it that way) except what the soul freely chooses.

The doctrine of "immediate mercy" <note>original: "immediate mercy"; the belief that God grants salvation instantly upon a profession of faith without any inner change or "regeneration" of the person's character</note>, based on a presumed faith where the soul is not <term>regenerated</term> <note>original: "regenerated"; meaning spiritually reborn or internally transformed</note>, is a major error found in almost every church. If a simple, arbitrary act of mercy were enough, not a single soul would be lost, nor would one Devil remain in hell, for with **GOD** there is infinite mercy. However, mercy can only be administered according to His unchanging laws of right to souls that are actually capable of receiving it. Therefore, the purpose of mercy is to provide means suited to our nature as <term>free agents</term> <note>original: "free agents"; beings with the capacity to choose their own actions</note> to fit and prepare us for the reception of divine love or blessedness. Once we are prepared, that love flows into the soul as naturally and necessarily as the air and light flow into our houses.

<summary>This page argues that true spiritual salvation requires a free-willed transformation of the soul rather than a deathbed repentance driven by fear. It critiques the idea of "immediate mercy," suggesting that God's mercy works by helping individuals qualify themselves to receive His love through a change in character.</summary>
<keywords>regeneration, immediate mercy, free agents, divine love, repentance, spiritual transformation, nature of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 457 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that true spiritual transformation requires the active participation of the individual and cannot be forced by fear or granted as a simple legal pardon without internal change.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>A LETTER to the PUBLISHER.</header>

I do not hesitate to declare that every living person is granted the necessary means of salvation, according to the <term>dispensation</term> <note>original: "dispensation"; the specific spiritual context, religious system, or era of divine dealing a person lives under</note> they are in. Furthermore, every change and so-called "accident" of a person's life is among those means; these events are orchestrated to shape the mind and invite a person to choose. This allows them to freely desire the good and <term>eschew</term> <note>original: "eschew"; to deliberately avoid or abstain from</note> evil until doing so becomes a natural habit. At that point, all is right and safe, so long as the soul continues to rely on **GOD** for the supply of grace.

However, not even the angels know how many various workings of <term>providence</term> <note>original: "providence"; the protective care and guidance of God or nature as a spiritual power</note> go into producing this outcome. A thousand turns in life may be necessary to correct the influence of a single thought upon the will, just as more than a thousand nerves and fibers are used in every movement of the body. In this way, the methods **GOD** uses toward man to ensure that goodness aligns with his free will and choice are infinite. This is because He neither does nor can force freedom. Instead, the will is led through a thousand different paths, turns, objects, and motives, so that every impression made on the mind in favor of the good is absolutely compatible with free will. Life is one complicated scheme of providential events directed toward this goal; and yet, for many people, even all of this will not be enough.

<summary>The author explains that God uses every event in a person's life as a providential tool to guide their free will toward goodness without using force. This complex process aims to make virtuous living a natural habit, though the author notes that some individuals still resist this divine guidance.</summary>
<keywords>free will, providence, salvation, grace, dispensation, divine orchestration, habits of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 458 ---
<meta>This page argues that true spiritual transformation is a process of the soul's free choice rather than a result of last-minute fear or an arbitrary act of divine mercy. It establishes that for salvation to be genuine, the soul must be "regenerated" or fundamentally changed so it can naturally receive God's love.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
# A LETTER to the PUBLISHER.

There is little room for hope at the end of life when the whole of that life has been habitually wrong before. Evil must be cleared away, and goodness must take shape in the soul so that it becomes the soul's own choice and its prevailing principle. This must happen in a season of freedom, when the mind is not biased by fear and dread, for a time of fear is no time of liberty. Therefore, when the pressure of fear is taken off, the soul—like a spring—returns to its former state. Nothing abides or is incorporated (if one may put it this way) into the soul except what the soul itself chooses.

The doctrine of immediate mercy based on a supposed faith, where the soul is not <term>regenerated</term> <note>original: "regenerated"; spiritually reborn or morally transformed through a change of heart</note>, is a major error of almost every church. If an arbitrary act of mercy were sufficient, not a single soul would be lost, nor would one devil remain in hell, for with **GOD** there is infinite mercy. But mercy can only be administered according to His <term>immutable</term> <note>original: "immutable"; unchanging over time or unable to be changed</note> laws of right to souls capable of receiving it. Therefore, the role of mercy is to provide means suited to the nature of <term>free agents</term> <note>original: "free agents"; beings with the capacity to choose their own actions</note> to fit and qualify them for the reception of divine love or blessedness. When they are so qualified, that love flows into the soul as naturally and necessarily as air and light flow into our houses.

<summary>The author argues that deathbed repentance is often invalid because it is born of fear rather than free choice, and that God's mercy requires a soul to be morally prepared to receive it.</summary>
<keywords>regeneration, free will, divine mercy, repentance, spiritual growth, salvation, laws of righteousness</keywords>

--- Page 459 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 48, explaining that salvation is a process of aligning the human will with goodness through the subtle guidance of divine providence, rather than through force.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>A LETTER to the PUBLISHER.</header>

I do not hesitate to state that every person living is granted the necessary means of salvation, according to the <term>dispensation</term> <note>original: "dispensation"; the specific religious system or set of circumstances under which a person lives</note> they are in. Every change and accident—as we call them—of a person's life is among those means. These events are so designed as to shape the mind and invite the person's choice, so that they may freely choose good and <term>eschew</term> <note>original: "eschew"; to deliberately avoid or abstain from</note> evil until doing so becomes a natural habit. Then, all is right and safe, as long as the soul depends upon **GOD** for a supply of grace. 

But even the angels do not know how many various workings of <term>providence</term> <note>original: "providence"; the protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power</note> go into producing this result. A thousand turns in life may be necessary to correct just one thought and its influence on the will, just as more than a thousand nerves and fibers are used in every movement of the body. 

In this way, **GOD’s** methods of making goodness consistent with a person's free will and choice are infinite; for He neither does nor can force a free being. Thus, the will is led by a thousand different paths, turns, objects, and motives, so that all the impressions made on the mind in favor of good are entirely compatible with free will. Life is one complicated scheme of <term>providences</term> <note>original: "providences"; specific instances of divine intervention or guidance</note> intended for this purpose; and yet, even this will not be enough for many people.

<summary>The author explains that God uses every event in a person's life as a subtle tool to encourage them to choose good over evil. This complex process of "providence" is designed to respect human free will while slowly shaping the soul into a state where goodness becomes habitual.</summary>
<keywords>salvation, free will, providence, grace, habit, divine guidance, choice</keywords>

--- Page 460 ---
<meta>This page concludes the letter from the previous page, shifting from the general workings of divine providence to the specific nature of true faith and the author's personal reflections on social isolation among believers.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>A LETTER to the PUBLISHER.</header>

The Apostle’s expression, "*No man can say that Jesus is the* LORD, *except by the Holy Spirit*," <note>original: "No man calleth Jesus LORD, but by the holy Ghost"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 12:3</note> does not refer to the mere physical act of the tongue on its own. Rather, it refers to the tongue only when it expresses the language of a heart rooted in divine faith and love. In that case, the essence of the confession comes from the **LORD** and is directed to the **LORD**, and not from man; for in man, in his own natural state, dwells no good thing.

I am quite a solitary person myself. It seems to me to be according to the general order of providence <note>original: "providence"; the protective care of God</note> that Christian friends are often separated, as we see is almost always the case. Perhaps this is because—either through a concern for social status <note>original: "human respects"; an over-reliance on the opinions or approval of others</note> on one hand, or through human weaknesses on the other—they might become obstacles to one another, and so experience a loss of true spiritual fellowship.

I intend to be in town on *Monday, November 6*; and should your own wishes or business lead you to *London* around that time, I shall have the happiness of meeting with you. I am,

->My dear SIR,<-

->*Your truly affectionate Friend, etc.*<-

### T H E E N D.

<summary>The author concludes the letter by stating that true confession of faith must come from a heart guided by the Holy Spirit and reflects on why God often keeps spiritual friends apart to avoid mutual distractions. </summary>
<keywords>Holy Spirit, faith, divine providence, Christian fellowship, solitude, London, 1 Corinthians 12:3</keywords>
